> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Physiology, interpretation and clinical reasoning are reliable at intern level. **Every reference range, dose, threshold, timing interval and scoring system carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.**
> Verify against **eTG Endocrinology**, the **Australian Medicines Handbook**, and — for pregnancy — obstetric endocrine guidance. **Trimester-specific TSH reference ranges are assay- and laboratory-specific and must come from your own laboratory.**

---

## 0.1 Thyroid Function Tests — Interpretation

**A/P:** Hypothalamic TRH → pituitary **TSH** → thyroid **T4 (the main secreted hormone, largely a prohormone) and T3 (the active hormone)** → negative feedback on the pituitary and hypothalamus. Most T3 is produced peripherally by deiodination of T4.

> [!tip] Why TSH is the most sensitive test
> **The relationship between free T4 and TSH is log-linear: a small change in free T4 produces a large, amplified change in TSH.**
> **So TSH becomes abnormal before free T4 leaves the reference range** — which is why it is the screening test of choice in primary thyroid disease, and why **subclinical disease (abnormal TSH, normal free T4) is a real biochemical entity rather than an artefact.**

> [!info] The patterns
> | TSH | Free T4 | Interpretation |
> |---|---|---|
> | **High** | **Low** | **Primary hypothyroidism** |
> | **High** | Normal | **Subclinical hypothyroidism** |
> | **Low** | **High** (or high T3) | **Primary hyperthyroidism** |
> | **Low** | Normal | **Subclinical hyperthyroidism** |
> | **Low or normal** | **Low** | **CENTRAL (secondary/tertiary) hypothyroidism** — pituitary or hypothalamic |
> | **High** | **High** | TSH-secreting adenoma, thyroid hormone resistance, **assay interference**, or recent non-adherence with a catch-up dose |
> `UNVERIFIED — all reference ranges.`

> [!danger] TSH is unreliable in central hypothyroidism — and the trap that follows
> **In pituitary or hypothalamic disease the TSH may be low, normal or even mildly raised despite a genuinely low free T4**, because the TSH produced is biologically inactive. **Relying on TSH alone will miss it.**
> **A low free T4 with a non-elevated TSH is central hypothyroidism until proven otherwise, and it demands assessment of the OTHER pituitary axes** — particularly **cortisol.** Cross-refer [[I4]].
> **This matters because starting thyroxine in a patient with undiagnosed adrenal insufficiency can precipitate an adrenal crisis** — thyroid hormone increases cortisol clearance and metabolic demand. **Always exclude and, if present, treat adrenal insufficiency BEFORE starting thyroxine.** This is a high-yield safety point.

> [!danger] Do not check thyroid function in an acutely unwell inpatient without a specific indication
> **Non-thyroidal illness syndrome ("sick euthyroid")** occurs in any significant acute illness: **T3 falls first, then T4 falls, and TSH is variable — often low during the illness and REBOUNDING HIGH during recovery.**
> **The results are uninterpretable, they do not reflect thyroid disease, and treating them causes harm.** A raised TSH in a recovering inpatient is far more likely to be recovery from non-thyroidal illness than new hypothyroidism.
> **Test when thyroid disease is genuinely suspected, and repeat abnormal results after recovery** rather than acting on them.

> [!warning] Interference and drug effects that produce spurious results
> **· Biotin supplements — high-dose biotin interferes with many immunoassays and produces a pattern that can mimic Graves disease (low TSH, high free T4).** **Ask about supplements**, which patients do not consider "medications". Biotin is in many hair, skin and nail products.
> **· Heterophile antibodies and macro-TSH** — laboratory artefacts producing discordant results. If the biochemistry does not match the patient, discuss with the laboratory.
> **· Oestrogen (pregnancy, the pill, HRT) raises thyroxine-binding globulin → raised TOTAL T4 with a normal FREE T4.** This is why **free** hormone levels are measured.
> **· Drugs affecting the axis:** **amiodarone** (both hypo- and hyperthyroidism — see 0.6), **lithium** (hypothyroidism and goitre), **corticosteroids and dopamine** (suppress TSH), **immune checkpoint inhibitors** (thyroiditis — cross-refer [[A5]] 0.7), phenytoin and carbamazepine, and **tyrosine kinase inhibitors.**

**Ix:** **TSH as the first-line test in a stable outpatient** (*why:* the most sensitive marker of primary thyroid dysfunction; *what:* the pattern above). **Free T4, and free T3 where hyperthyroidism is suspected** (*why:* free T4 confirms the degree of dysfunction, and **T3 toxicosis exists in which T3 is raised with a normal free T4 — so a suppressed TSH with a normal free T4 warrants a T3**; *what:* free hormone levels). **Thyroid autoantibodies** (*why:* **anti-TPO supports Hashimoto thyroiditis and predicts progression from subclinical to overt hypothyroidism; TSH receptor antibodies (TRAb) confirm Graves disease and predict neonatal thyrotoxicosis in pregnancy**; *what:* positive antibodies). Medication and supplement history including biotin (*why:* interference and drug-induced dysfunction; *what:* culprit).

---

## 0.2 Hypothyroidism

**R/Causes:** **Autoimmune (Hashimoto) thyroiditis — the commonest cause in iodine-replete countries such as Australia**, often with a goitre and positive anti-TPO antibodies, and clustering with other autoimmune conditions. **Iatrogenic** — after thyroidectomy, radioiodine, or external beam radiotherapy to the neck. **Drugs** — amiodarone, lithium, checkpoint inhibitors, tyrosine kinase inhibitors. **Iodine deficiency** — the commonest cause worldwide. **Congenital.** **Central.** **Transient** — postpartum thyroiditis and the hypothyroid phase of subacute thyroiditis.

**S/Smx:** **Fatigue, cold intolerance, weight gain, constipation, dry skin, hair thinning and loss of the outer eyebrow, hoarse voice, menorrhagia, low mood, cognitive slowing, myalgia and carpal tunnel syndrome.**
Signs: **bradycardia, delayed relaxation phase of the deep tendon reflexes** (a genuinely useful and specific sign), coarse dry skin, non-pitting periorbital and peripheral oedema, goitre, pericardial and pleural effusions, and hypothermia in severe cases.

> [!tip] The biochemical fellow-travellers
> **Hyponatraemia · hyperlipidaemia · raised creatine kinase (which can be markedly elevated and mistaken for a primary myopathy) · normocytic or macrocytic anaemia · raised prolactin.**
> **Unexplained hyperlipidaemia, a raised CK, or hyponatraemia should prompt a TSH** — it is a cheap test that occasionally explains everything.

> [!warning] Levothyroxine — the practical points that determine whether it works
> **· Take it once daily, on an empty stomach, ideally 30–60 minutes before food** — absorption is significantly reduced by food.
> **· Separate it by several hours from calcium, iron, magnesium, proton pump inhibitors, cholestyramine and soy**, all of which impair absorption. **This is the commonest reason a patient's TSH will not normalise despite escalating doses.**
> **· Start at a low dose in the elderly and in ischaemic heart disease**, and increase slowly — a full replacement dose can precipitate angina, arrhythmia or infarction.
> **· Recheck after an adequate interval** — the axis takes weeks to re-equilibrate, and checking too early leads to unnecessary dose changes.
> `UNVERIFIED — starting doses, dose adjustment increments, the recheck interval, and target TSH.`

> [!danger] Do not over-treat — a suppressed TSH is not a better result
> **Over-replacement causes atrial fibrillation and osteoporosis**, and the risk is greatest in **older people and postmenopausal women** — exactly the population most often over-treated.
> **A suppressed TSH on thyroxine is a reason to reduce the dose**, not a sign of good control.

> [!tip] Persistent symptoms with normal thyroid function
> A common and difficult scenario. **Once the TSH is genuinely in range and absorption is optimised, look for another explanation** — **anaemia and iron deficiency, coeliac disease, vitamin D and B12 deficiency, depression, obstructive sleep apnoea, adrenal insufficiency, or another autoimmune condition** (which cluster with Hashimoto disease).
> **Combination liothyronine (T3) and thyroxine therapy is not routinely recommended**, and desiccated thyroid extract is not recommended — but patients frequently ask about both, so it is worth being able to explain the position without dismissiveness. `UNVERIFIED — the current position on combination therapy.`

> [!info] Subclinical hypothyroidism
> Raised TSH with a normal free T4. **Treatment is considered where the TSH is above a defined threshold, where the patient is symptomatic, where anti-TPO antibodies are positive (predicting progression), and — importantly — in pregnancy or when planning pregnancy.**
> **Otherwise, many cases are managed with observation and repeat testing**, since a proportion normalise spontaneously. `UNVERIFIED — the TSH threshold for treatment and the monitoring interval.`

**Ix:** TSH and free T4 (*why:* diagnosis and severity; *what:* the pattern). **Anti-TPO antibodies** (*why:* confirms autoimmune aetiology and predicts progression in subclinical disease; *what:* positive). FBC, lipids, UEC, CK (*why:* the associated abnormalities, several of which resolve with treatment; *what:* anaemia, hyperlipidaemia, hyponatraemia, raised CK). Coeliac serology and other autoimmune screening where clinically indicated (*why:* autoimmune conditions cluster — type 1 diabetes, coeliac disease, pernicious anaemia, Addison disease; *what:* associated autoimmunity). **Morning cortisol where central hypothyroidism is suspected** (*why:* to exclude adrenal insufficiency before starting thyroxine; *what:* low cortisol — cross-refer [[I4]]).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Only in myxoedema coma — see 0.4.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Levothyroxine with the administration advice above, titrated to a target TSH.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Annual TFTs once stable, dose review in pregnancy (see 0.6), and awareness that **requirements change with weight, pregnancy, oestrogen therapy and interacting drugs.**

---

## 0.3 Hyperthyroidism and Thyrotoxicosis

**R/Causes:**
**· Graves disease** — the commonest, an autoimmune condition with **TSH receptor stimulating antibodies**, producing a **diffuse goitre with a bruit**, and — uniquely — **ophthalmopathy and pretibial myxoedema.**
**· Toxic multinodular goitre and toxic adenoma** — autonomous nodules, more common in older patients.
**· Thyroiditis** — **subacute (de Quervain): painful, tender thyroid, post-viral, with a raised ESR**; **postpartum**; and **silent/painless.** All are **destructive**, releasing preformed hormone.
**· Iodine-induced (Jod-Basedow)** — **amiodarone, iodinated contrast**, iodine supplements, in a susceptible nodular thyroid.
**· Factitious** — exogenous thyroid hormone, sometimes for weight loss.
**· hCG-mediated** — hyperemesis gravidarum, molar pregnancy.
**· Rare** — struma ovarii, TSH-secreting pituitary adenoma, metastatic thyroid cancer.

> [!danger] The most important distinction: thyroiditis versus true hyperthyroidism
> **Thyroiditis is DESTRUCTIVE — preformed hormone leaks from a damaged gland. Nothing is being synthesised.**
> **Therefore ANTITHYROID DRUGS DO NOT WORK IN THYROIDITIS**, because there is no excess synthesis to block. Giving carbimazole is futile and exposes the patient to agranulocytosis risk for no benefit.
> **Thyroiditis is self-limiting**, typically running through a thyrotoxic phase, then a hypothyroid phase, then usually recovery. **Treatment is symptomatic — a beta-blocker, and analgesia or corticosteroids for painful subacute thyroiditis.**
> **How to distinguish:**
> **· Radionuclide uptake scan — the definitive test. Uptake is HIGH in Graves disease and toxic nodules (the gland is actively making hormone), and LOW or ABSENT in thyroiditis and in factitious thyrotoxicosis (the gland is not).**
> **· TSH receptor antibodies (TRAb)** — positive in Graves disease.
> **· Clinical** — pain and tenderness with raised inflammatory markers suggests subacute thyroiditis; eye signs and a diffuse bruit suggest Graves.

**S/Smx:** **Weight loss despite increased appetite, heat intolerance, sweating, palpitations, anxiety and irritability, tremor, frequent loose stools, oligomenorrhoea, proximal muscle weakness, insomnia.**
Signs: **tachycardia or atrial fibrillation, fine tremor, warm moist skin, lid retraction and lid lag** (which occur in any thyrotoxicosis), goitre with or without bruit, **proximal myopathy, brisk reflexes, onycholysis.**

> [!danger] Apathetic thyrotoxicosis in the elderly
> **Older patients frequently present WITHOUT the hyperkinetic features** — instead with **weight loss, atrial fibrillation, heart failure, depression, apathy and proximal weakness.**
> **Check thyroid function in every new atrial fibrillation, in unexplained weight loss in an older person, and in unexplained heart failure.** This is one of the more reliably missed endocrine diagnoses.

> [!warning] Graves ophthalmopathy is a separate disease process
> **Proptosis, lid retraction, chemosis, conjunctival injection, diplopia from extraocular muscle involvement, and — in severe disease — corneal exposure and compressive OPTIC NEUROPATHY with reduced acuity and colour vision.**
> **It follows its own course, independent of the biochemical thyroid status**, and treating the thyrotoxicosis does not necessarily improve the eyes.
> **Three things matter clinically:**
> **1. SMOKING is the strongest modifiable risk factor and markedly worsens it. Cessation is a core part of management, not a general health suggestion.**
> **2. RADIOIODINE CAN WORSEN eye disease**, so it is used with caution and often with corticosteroid cover in patients with active ophthalmopathy.
> **3. Reduced visual acuity, colour desaturation or corneal ulceration is SIGHT-THREATENING and requires urgent ophthalmology.**

> [!danger] Antithyroid drugs — the agranulocytosis counselling is mandatory
> **Carbimazole (and thiamazole) and propylthiouracil can cause AGRANULOCYTOSIS**, which is rare, idiosyncratic, unpredictable, and potentially fatal.
> **Every patient must be told explicitly: "If you develop a sore throat, mouth ulcers, or a fever, STOP the tablets immediately and get a full blood count the same day."**
> **Routine monitoring of the white cell count does not reliably detect it** — the counselling is the safeguard, and **documenting that you gave it is important.**
> Also: **hepatotoxicity — more severe with propylthiouracil, which can cause fulminant hepatic failure**, which is why carbimazole is generally preferred outside the first trimester of pregnancy.
> **Carbimazole is associated with a characteristic embryopathy, so PROPYLTHIOURACIL is preferred in the FIRST TRIMESTER, with a switch back thereafter.** Cross-refer 0.6.
> `UNVERIFIED — doses, monitoring, titration versus block-and-replace regimens, and the pregnancy switching points.`

**Ix:** TSH, free T4 and **free T3** (*why:* T3 toxicosis produces a suppressed TSH with a normal free T4, and is missed if T3 is not measured; *what:* the pattern). **TSH receptor antibodies (TRAb)** (*why:* confirms Graves disease, avoids the need for a scan in many cases, and **predicts fetal and neonatal thyrotoxicosis in pregnancy**; *what:* positive). **Radionuclide uptake scan** (*why:* **the test that distinguishes hyperthyroidism from thyroiditis, which determines whether antithyroid drugs will work at all**; *what:* diffuse high uptake in Graves, focal in a toxic nodule, absent in thyroiditis — **contraindicated in pregnancy and breastfeeding**). ESR and CRP (*why:* markedly raised in subacute thyroiditis; *what:* elevation). **ECG** (*why:* atrial fibrillation is common and may be the presenting feature; *what:* AF, tachycardia). LFT and FBC before starting antithyroid drugs (*why:* baseline before hepatotoxic and marrow-toxic agents; *what:* baseline). Bone density in longstanding or subclinical disease (*why:* thyrotoxicosis causes accelerated bone loss; *what:* reduced density). Pregnancy test (*why:* changes the agent, contraindicates radioiodine, and hCG-mediated thyrotoxicosis is a differential; *what:* positive).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
**Beta-blocker for symptomatic relief** — it treats the adrenergic symptoms rapidly while definitive therapy takes weeks. Thyroid storm per 0.4.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
**Antithyroid drugs** with agranulocytosis counselling, then **definitive therapy — radioiodine or surgery** — depending on cause, patient preference, eye disease, pregnancy plans and goitre size.
**Surgical risks to counsel: recurrent laryngeal nerve injury (voice change) and hypoparathyroidism with hypocalcaemia.**
**Radioiodine: contraindicated in pregnancy and breastfeeding, requires avoidance of pregnancy for a defined period afterwards, may worsen eye disease, and usually results in eventual hypothyroidism requiring lifelong replacement** — which should be presented as an expected outcome rather than a complication.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Lifelong monitoring after any definitive treatment**, because hypothyroidism develops in most and may appear years later. **Smoking cessation, particularly with eye disease.** Bone and cardiac assessment.

---

## 0.4 Thyroid Emergencies

> [!danger] Thyroid storm — the sequence of drugs matters
> **Decompensated thyrotoxicosis with organ dysfunction: fever, marked tachycardia or tachyarrhythmia, heart failure, agitation, delirium or coma, vomiting, diarrhoea and jaundice.**
> **Precipitants: infection, surgery (especially thyroid surgery in an unprepared patient), trauma, radioiodine, an iodine load including contrast, childbirth, diabetic ketoacidosis, and abrupt cessation of antithyroid medication.**
> **This is a CLINICAL diagnosis — the biochemistry is no different from uncomplicated thyrotoxicosis. Do not wait for thyroid function tests to treat.** Scoring systems such as Burch-Wartofsky support the assessment. `UNVERIFIED — the score.`
>
> **The treatment sequence, and why the order matters:**
> **1. Beta-blocker** — controls the adrenergic manifestations and, at high dose, reduces peripheral T4 to T3 conversion.
> **2. Antithyroid drug** — blocks new hormone synthesis.
> **3. IODINE — and this must be given at least an hour AFTER the antithyroid drug.** Iodine blocks hormone release (the Wolff-Chaikoff effect), **but if given before synthesis is blocked it provides substrate and can WORSEN the storm.** **Getting this order wrong is a genuine and examinable error.**
> **4. Corticosteroid** — reduces peripheral T4 to T3 conversion and treats the relative adrenal insufficiency of severe thyrotoxicosis.
> **5. Supportive care and treatment of the precipitant** — cooling, fluids, and **treating the infection or other trigger, which is frequently the thing that actually resolves it.**
> **Avoid aspirin**, which displaces thyroid hormone from binding proteins and raises the free fraction.
> `UNVERIFIED — all agents and doses.`

> [!danger] Myxoedema coma — give hydrocortisone with the thyroid hormone
> **Decompensated hypothyroidism: hypothermia (which may be profound and missed with a standard thermometer), bradycardia, hypoventilation with hypercapnia, hypotension, hyponatraemia, hypoglycaemia, and reduced consciousness progressing to coma.**
> **Precipitants: infection, cold exposure, sedatives and opioids, stroke, myocardial infarction, and non-adherence with thyroxine — often in an older person living alone in winter.**
> **Treatment: intravenous thyroid hormone, supportive care with passive rewarming, ventilatory support, cautious fluid and glucose management, and treatment of the precipitant.**
> **Give intravenous HYDROCORTISONE before or with the thyroid hormone** — coexisting adrenal insufficiency is common, and thyroid hormone increases cortisol clearance and metabolic demand, so **giving thyroid hormone alone can precipitate an adrenal crisis.** This mirrors the point in 0.1 and is the same physiology.
> **Rewarm passively rather than actively** — active external rewarming causes peripheral vasodilatation and cardiovascular collapse.
> **Mortality remains high.** ICU-level care.
> `UNVERIFIED — thyroid hormone preparation, loading and maintenance doses, and hydrocortisone dosing.`

**Ix:** **Treat on clinical grounds — the investigations confirm afterwards.** TSH, free T4 and free T3 (*why:* confirmatory; *what:* the pattern). **Cortisol, taken before hydrocortisone if it costs no delay** (*why:* coexisting adrenal insufficiency; *what:* inappropriately low cortisol). **Septic screen and a search for the precipitant** (*why:* infection is the commonest trigger for both, and treating it is essential; *what:* source). Glucose, UEC, sodium, VBG (*why:* hypoglycaemia, hyponatraemia and hypercapnia in myxoedema coma; *what:* derangement). **Core temperature with a low-reading thermometer** (*why:* standard thermometers do not read low enough to detect the degree of hypothermia; *what:* accurate core temperature). ECG and cardiac monitoring (*why:* arrhythmia in both conditions; *what:* AF, bradyarrhythmia). CK (*why:* markedly raised in myxoedema; *what:* elevation).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
As per the sequences above. **ICU involvement, endocrinology involvement, and treatment of the precipitant.**

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Transition to oral therapy and definitive management of the underlying thyroid disease once stable.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Address the reason it happened — most often non-adherence, an unrecognised precipitant, or inadequate preparation before surgery or radioiodine.** Education and follow-up.

---

## 0.5 Thyroid Nodule and Goitre

> [!warning] Thyroid nodules are extremely common and the great majority are benign
> Prevalence rises with age, and **incidental nodules are found frequently on imaging performed for other reasons** — carotid ultrasound, neck CT, PET.
> **The clinical task is to identify the small proportion that are malignant or functional, without subjecting everyone to biopsy.**

> [!tip] The assessment pathway, and why TFTs come first
> **1. Thyroid function tests FIRST.**
> **2. If the TSH is SUPPRESSED → radionuclide uptake scan.** A **"hot" (autonomously functioning) nodule is almost never malignant**, so **it does not need fine needle aspiration** — it needs treatment for hyperthyroidism.
> **3. If the TSH is normal or raised → ultrasound with sonographic risk stratification**, and then **fine needle aspiration based on the size and the sonographic features combined**, not on size alone.
> **Doing the TFT first is what prevents an unnecessary biopsy of a hot nodule.**

> [!tip] Sonographic features suggesting malignancy
> **· Microcalcifications · a "taller than wide" shape · irregular or infiltrative margins · marked hypoechogenicity · extrathyroidal extension · associated abnormal cervical lymph nodes.**
> **Purely cystic lesions and spongiform nodules are almost always benign.**
> Risk stratification systems (TIRADS and equivalents) formalise this and determine the FNA threshold. `UNVERIFIED — the systems, categories and size thresholds.`

> [!warning] Clinical features raising concern
> **· Age extremes — under 20 or over 70 · male sex · a history of childhood head or neck irradiation · a family history of thyroid cancer or MEN2 · rapid growth · a hard, fixed nodule · hoarseness from recurrent laryngeal nerve involvement · cervical lymphadenopathy · compressive symptoms.**
> **New hoarseness with a thyroid nodule is malignancy until proven otherwise** and warrants urgent assessment including laryngoscopy. Cross-refer [[D7]] 0.4.

> [!info] The thyroid cancers, and the one FNA cannot diagnose
> **· Papillary — the commonest.** Spreads via lymphatics to cervical nodes. **Excellent prognosis.** Associated with radiation exposure.
> **· Follicular** — spreads haematogenously to bone and lung. **CRITICAL POINT: fine needle aspiration CANNOT distinguish follicular adenoma from follicular carcinoma**, because the distinction rests on capsular and vascular invasion, which requires the whole lesion. **A "follicular neoplasm" on cytology therefore requires diagnostic surgical excision.**
> **· Medullary** — arises from **parafollicular C cells**, secretes **CALCITONIN**, and is associated with **MEN2**, so a diagnosis mandates **RET proto-oncogene testing and family screening**, and **phaeochromocytoma must be excluded before any surgery.** Cross-refer [[I4]] and [[B2]] 0.4.
> **· Anaplastic** — elderly, **rapidly enlarging hard mass with compressive symptoms**, very poor prognosis.
> **· Thyroid lymphoma** — arising on a background of **Hashimoto thyroiditis**, presenting as a rapidly enlarging goitre.
> Cytology is reported using the **Bethesda system**, which stratifies malignancy risk and guides management. `UNVERIFIED — the categories and their associated risks.`

> [!tip] Goitre and compressive symptoms
> **Diffuse** (Graves, Hashimoto, iodine deficiency, physiological in puberty and pregnancy) or **multinodular.**
> **Retrosternal extension** produces compressive symptoms — **dysphagia, dyspnoea, stridor, and superior vena cava obstruction.**
> **Pemberton sign — facial plethora, distress and venous distension on raising both arms above the head** — indicates thoracic inlet obstruction and is a simple, useful bedside test. Cross-refer [[B6]] 0.4.
> **Assess the airway**: stridor, tracheal deviation, and — where compression is suspected — **CT and flow-volume loops** rather than reassurance. Cross-refer [[A2]] 0.3.

**Ix:** **TSH first** (*why:* determines whether the next step is a scan or an ultrasound, and prevents unnecessary biopsy of a hot nodule; *what:* suppressed, normal or raised). **Thyroid ultrasound with risk stratification** (*why:* characterises the nodule and assesses cervical nodes, and the features determine the FNA threshold; *what:* size, sonographic features, lymphadenopathy). **Radionuclide uptake scan if TSH suppressed** (*why:* identifies an autonomously functioning nodule; *what:* hot or cold). **Fine needle aspiration per size and risk criteria** (*why:* the diagnostic test for most nodules, with the follicular caveat; *what:* Bethesda category). **Calcitonin where medullary carcinoma is suspected or there is a family history** (*why:* the tumour marker, and it prompts RET testing and phaeochromocytoma exclusion; *what:* elevated calcitonin). CT neck and thorax **without iodinated contrast where radioiodine treatment is anticipated** (*why:* an iodine load interferes with subsequent radioiodine uptake and treatment for months — **this is an easily made and consequential error**; *what:* retrosternal extension, compression, nodes). Laryngoscopy where there is voice change or before thyroid surgery (*why:* documents pre-existing cord function; *what:* cord palsy).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Airway assessment where there is stridor or significant compression.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Surveillance for benign nodules, surgery for malignancy, indeterminate cytology, compressive symptoms or a toxic nodule, and **radioiodine or antithyroid drugs for functional nodules.**

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Lifelong thyroxine after total thyroidectomy** — and **TSH suppression rather than simple replacement in differentiated thyroid cancer**, to reduce recurrence. Thyroglobulin monitoring as a tumour marker. Family screening in medullary carcinoma.

---

## 0.6 Thyroid Disease in Pregnancy and Special Situations

> [!danger] Pregnancy changes the physiology, the reference ranges and the doses
> **· hCG structurally resembles TSH and weakly stimulates the TSH receptor, so TSH is PHYSIOLOGICALLY SUPPRESSED in the first trimester.** A low TSH in early pregnancy is often normal, and applying non-pregnant reference ranges over-diagnoses hyperthyroidism.
> **· Trimester-specific reference ranges must be used**, and they are assay-specific. `UNVERIFIED — obtain from your laboratory.`
> **· Oestrogen raises thyroxine-binding globulin**, so **total** T4 rises while free T4 remains appropriate.
> **· Thyroxine requirements INCREASE substantially in pregnancy — commonly by around a third.** **A woman on thyroxine who becomes pregnant needs her dose increased as soon as pregnancy is confirmed, without waiting for a test**, with early and frequent monitoring. **Many services advise the woman in advance to increase her own dose on a positive pregnancy test.** `UNVERIFIED — the recommended dose increase and monitoring schedule.`
> **· Untreated or under-treated maternal hypothyroidism is associated with miscarriage, pre-eclampsia, preterm birth, and impaired fetal neurodevelopment** — fetal thyroid hormone is entirely maternal in the first trimester.

> [!warning] Hyperthyroidism in pregnancy
> **· Gestational transient thyrotoxicosis** — hCG-mediated, associated with **hyperemesis gravidarum**, self-limiting, **TRAb negative, no eye signs, no goitre.** Usually needs no antithyroid treatment.
> **· Graves disease** — needs treatment. **Propylthiouracil is preferred in the FIRST trimester because of carbimazole embryopathy, with a switch to carbimazole thereafter because of propylthiouracil hepatotoxicity.**
> **· Use the LOWEST effective dose** — antithyroid drugs cross the placenta and can cause fetal hypothyroidism and goitre.
> **· Radioiodine is absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy and breastfeeding.**
> **· TRAb crosses the placenta and can cause FETAL AND NEONATAL THYROTOXICOSIS — even in a mother who is euthyroid after previous definitive treatment**, because the antibodies persist. **TRAb should be measured in pregnancy in women with current or past Graves disease**, and a high titre prompts fetal monitoring and neonatal assessment.
> `UNVERIFIED — trimester switching points, dosing, and TRAb monitoring recommendations.`

> [!tip] Postpartum thyroiditis
> Occurs in a meaningful proportion of women in the year after delivery, more commonly in those with **positive anti-TPO antibodies or type 1 diabetes.**
> Classically a **thyrotoxic phase, then a hypothyroid phase, then recovery** — though any phase may occur alone.
> **It is frequently misattributed to postnatal depression, normal postpartum fatigue, or anxiety**, and the hypothyroid phase in particular overlaps heavily with those. **Check thyroid function in a woman with postnatal mood or fatigue symptoms.**
> A proportion develop permanent hypothyroidism, so follow-up is needed. Cross-refer [[O3]].

> [!danger] Amiodarone-induced thyroid dysfunction — two types, opposite treatments
> Amiodarone contains a large iodine load and is highly lipophilic with a very long half-life, so **effects persist for many months after cessation.**
> **· Amiodarone-induced HYPOthyroidism** — commoner in iodine-replete areas such as Australia, straightforward to treat with thyroxine, and **amiodarone can usually be continued.**
> **· Amiodarone-induced THYROTOXICOSIS type 1** — **iodine-induced excess synthesis in a pre-existing nodular or autonomous gland.** Treated with **antithyroid drugs.**
> **· Amiodarone-induced THYROTOXICOSIS type 2** — **a destructive thyroiditis.** Treated with **corticosteroids.**
> **Distinguishing type 1 from type 2 is genuinely difficult**, colour Doppler flow on ultrasound helps (increased in type 1, absent in type 2), and **mixed forms occur — so both treatments are frequently given together.** This is a specialist problem, and **stopping amiodarone does not produce rapid improvement because of the long half-life.**
> `UNVERIFIED — diagnostic criteria and treatment regimens.`

> [!tip] Other situations worth knowing
> **· Immune checkpoint inhibitor thyroiditis** — common, often a transient thyrotoxic phase followed by permanent hypothyroidism requiring lifelong replacement. Cross-refer [[A5]] 0.7.
> **· Lithium** — hypothyroidism and goitre; monitor thyroid function during therapy.
> **· Congenital hypothyroidism** — **detected by newborn screening (the heel prick test) in Australia**, and **early treatment prevents the profound intellectual disability that untreated congenital hypothyroidism causes.** This is one of the clearest examples of the value of newborn screening. Cross-refer [[M4]].
> **· Iodine** — Australia has had mandatory iodine fortification of bread, and **pregnant and breastfeeding women are recommended iodine supplementation.** `UNVERIFIED — current supplementation recommendations.`

**Ix:** **Trimester-specific TSH and free T4** (*why:* non-pregnant ranges misclassify; *what:* trimester-appropriate interpretation). **TRAb in women with current or previous Graves disease** (*why:* predicts fetal and neonatal thyrotoxicosis regardless of maternal thyroid status; *what:* titre). Anti-TPO antibodies (*why:* predicts postpartum thyroiditis and progression of subclinical hypothyroidism; *what:* positive). **Thyroid ultrasound with colour Doppler in amiodarone-induced thyrotoxicosis** (*why:* helps distinguish type 1 from type 2, which have opposite treatments; *what:* vascularity). **Radionuclide scanning is CONTRAINDICATED in pregnancy and breastfeeding** (*why:* fetal thyroid uptake and radiation; *what:* not to be performed). Fetal monitoring including growth and heart rate where TRAb is high (*why:* fetal thyrotoxicosis; *what:* tachycardia, goitre, growth restriction).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
As per the underlying condition, with pregnancy-appropriate agents.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Multidisciplinary management with obstetrics and endocrinology. **Increase thyroxine promptly on confirmation of pregnancy.**

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Postpartum thyroid function checks** in women with autoimmune thyroid disease. **Neonatal assessment where maternal TRAb is high.** Preconception optimisation for the next pregnancy.

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Fever versus hyperthermia and thyroid storm in the hyperthermia differential → [[A6]] 0.3 · Atrial fibrillation and the need to check TFTs → [[B3]] 0.4 · Heart failure and thyroid disease → [[CV-X]] 0.1 · Hyponatraemia and metabolic associations → [[F0.2]] and [[H3]] · Adrenal insufficiency and the pituitary axes → [[I4]] and [[F0.3]] 0.6 · Weight loss and lipids → [[I5]] · Osteoporosis and over-replacement → [[GER1]] 0.6 · Thyroid enlargement and airway compression → [[A2]] 0.3 · Hoarseness and recurrent laryngeal nerve → [[D7]] 0.4 and [[F3]] · Facial swelling and Pemberton sign → [[B6]] 0.4 · Checkpoint inhibitor endocrinopathy → [[A5]] 0.7 · Fatigue and its differential → [[B6]] 0.5 · Depression → [[N4]] · Postnatal mental health → [[O3]] · Newborn screening → [[M4]]

> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Pathophysiology, classification and clinical reasoning are reliable at intern level. **Every diagnostic threshold, HbA1c target, dose, screening interval and glycaemic cut-off carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.**
> **Diabetes pharmacotherapy has changed substantially** — SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 receptor agonists now have indications driven by cardiovascular and renal outcomes rather than glucose. Verify against **the RACGP *Management of type 2 diabetes: A handbook for general practice*, Diabetes Australia / ADS guidance, eTG Endocrinology, and PBS authority criteria**, which determine what is actually prescribable here.
>
> **DKA and hyperglycaemic emergencies are built in [[F0.2]] 0.2–0.4** and are cross-referenced rather than repeated.

---

## 0.1 Diagnosis and Classification

> [!tip] The diagnostic tests, and the rule that governs them
> Diabetes is diagnosed by **fasting plasma glucose, a two-hour value on oral glucose tolerance testing, HbA1c, or a random glucose in the presence of classic symptoms.**
> **In an ASYMPTOMATIC person, a single abnormal result is not sufficient — it must be confirmed on a separate occasion**, ideally with the same test.
> **In a SYMPTOMATIC person with an unequivocally raised random glucose, no confirmation is needed.**
> `UNVERIFIED — all diagnostic threshold values, and the separate thresholds defining impaired fasting glucose and impaired glucose tolerance.`

> [!danger] HbA1c is unreliable in several common situations
> It reflects average glycaemia over the lifespan of the red cell, so **anything that alters red cell turnover distorts it:**
> **· Anaemia of any cause, haemolysis, recent transfusion, and haemoglobinopathies** (thalassaemia and haemoglobin variants — relevant in Australia's diverse population).
> **· Chronic kidney disease** and erythropoietin therapy.
> **· Pregnancy.**
> **· Rapidly evolving hyperglycaemia — as in new type 1 diabetes — where HbA1c lags behind the actual glucose and can be misleadingly reassuring in a patient who is about to develop DKA.**
> **In these situations use glucose-based testing.**

> [!info] Classification — and why getting it right matters
> **· Type 1** — autoimmune beta cell destruction. **Antibodies (GAD, IA-2, ZnT8) positive, C-peptide low, ketosis-prone, requires insulin from diagnosis.**
> **· Type 2** — insulin resistance with progressive relative insulin deficiency.
> **· Gestational** — see 0.7.
> **· Other specific types** — **pancreatic (type 3c: chronic pancreatitis, pancreatectomy, cystic fibrosis, haemochromatosis — cross-refer [[C7]] 0.3), endocrinopathies (Cushing syndrome, acromegaly, phaeochromocytoma), drug-induced (corticosteroids, antipsychotics, immunosuppressants), and monogenic diabetes.**

> [!danger] Two forms adults are misclassified as
> **· LADA (latent autoimmune diabetes in adults)** — **adult onset, often lean, initially appearing to be type 2, but antibody-positive with progressive beta cell failure and early failure of oral agents.** **Suspect it in an adult diagnosed with "type 2" who is not overweight, has other autoimmune disease, or deteriorates rapidly despite treatment.** **Check antibodies and C-peptide.** Misclassification leads to DKA in someone who was thought not to need insulin.
> **· MODY (maturely-onset diabetes of the young)** — **young onset (often under 25), a strong family history across multiple generations, non-obese, antibody-negative, and not ketosis-prone.** It is monogenic. **Some subtypes respond very well to low-dose sulfonylureas rather than insulin, and one subtype needs no treatment at all** — so the diagnosis genuinely changes management and has implications for the whole family. It is frequently misdiagnosed as type 1 and the patient placed on insulin for years.
> **The practical rule: if the diabetes does not fit the expected phenotype, question the classification.**

> [!warning] The Australian context
> **Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people experience substantially higher diabetes prevalence, onset at younger ages, and higher rates of complications**, which is why **screening begins at a younger age in this population.** Cross-refer [[AU1]] and [[GER3]].
> **The National Diabetes Services Scheme (NDSS)** provides subsidised access to consumables — test strips, needles, and continuous glucose monitoring for eligible groups. **Registering a newly diagnosed patient is a concrete practical action**, and it is frequently overlooked at diagnosis. `UNVERIFIED — current NDSS eligibility and what is subsidised.`

**S/Smx:** Classic osmotic symptoms — **polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, blurred vision, fatigue, recurrent infections including candidiasis** — or asymptomatic detection on screening. **Type 1 presents more acutely, often with DKA; type 2 is frequently present for years before diagnosis, which is why complications may be present at the time of diagnosis.**

**Ix:** **Fasting glucose, HbA1c, or OGTT** (*why:* the diagnostic tests, with confirmation required in asymptomatic people; *what:* values against threshold). **Islet autoantibodies (GAD, IA-2, ZnT8) and C-peptide where classification is uncertain** (*why:* distinguishes type 1 and LADA from type 2, and misclassification risks DKA; *what:* positive antibodies, low C-peptide). **Ketones where hyperglycaemia is marked or the patient is unwell** (*why:* identifies impending or actual DKA — cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.3; *what:* beta-hydroxybutyrate). At diagnosis of type 2, a **complication and comorbidity screen — UEC and eGFR, urine albumin-creatinine ratio, lipids, LFT, blood pressure, retinal screening and foot examination** (*why:* **complications are frequently already present at diagnosis in type 2 because of the preceding asymptomatic period**; *what:* baseline complication status). TFTs and coeliac serology in type 1 (*why:* associated autoimmune conditions cluster; *what:* associated disease). Genetic testing where MODY is suspected (*why:* changes treatment and has family implications; *what:* pathogenic variant).

---

## 0.2 Type 2 Diabetes — Management

> [!danger] Individualise the glycaemic target — tight control is not universally good
> **A tighter target is appropriate in: younger patients, newly diagnosed disease, long life expectancy, no established cardiovascular disease, and where it can be achieved without hypoglycaemia.**
> **A looser target is appropriate in: older and frail patients, limited life expectancy, established complications, hypoglycaemia unawareness, and where treatment burden outweighs benefit.**
> **Over-treating an elderly frail patient causes hypoglycaemia, falls, fractures and death, and the benefit of tight control accrues over a timeframe they may not have.** **Deintensifying diabetes treatment in the elderly is an active and appropriate intervention**, not neglect. Cross-refer [[GER1]] 0.4.
> `UNVERIFIED — the HbA1c targets for each group.`

> [!tip] Lifestyle first, and remission is now a realistic goal
> **Substantial weight loss can induce remission of type 2 diabetes**, particularly early in the disease — through intensive dietary intervention or bariatric surgery. **This is a genuine change in how the disease is framed: it is not necessarily lifelong and progressive.**
> Alongside: physical activity (both aerobic and resistance), smoking cessation, alcohol reduction, and sleep — **including treating obstructive sleep apnoea, which is very common and worsens glycaemic control.**

> [!info] Pharmacotherapy — and the shift from "treat the number" to "treat the patient"
> **· Metformin — first-line for most.** Reduces hepatic glucose output. **Practical points: gastrointestinal side effects (start low, take with food, extended-release helps) · long-term use causes VITAMIN B12 DEFICIENCY, which should be checked periodically and is a genuinely missed cause of neuropathy in diabetics · withhold in acute illness, before contrast, and with significant renal impairment.** Lactic acidosis is rare but occurs with accumulation — cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.5.
> **· SGLT2 inhibitors — now prescribed for cardiovascular and renal protection, independent of glycaemic need.** **Indicated in established cardiovascular disease, heart failure and chronic kidney disease regardless of HbA1c.** Cross-refer [[CV-X]] 0.3 and [[H3]].
> **Risks to counsel: euglycaemic DKA (cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.3), genital mycotic infection, volume depletion, and rare Fournier gangrene.** **Sick day rules and perioperative withholding are essential.**
> **· GLP-1 receptor agonists** — weight loss and cardiovascular benefit. Gastrointestinal side effects are common. **They delay gastric emptying, and there is emerging concern about aspiration risk during anaesthesia and endoscopy, prompting recommendations to withhold before procedures.** `UNVERIFIED — the withholding recommendations, which are recent and evolving.`
> **· Sulfonylureas** — effective and cheap, but **cause hypoglycaemia, which is prolonged and dangerous in the elderly and in renal impairment.** Longer-acting agents are worse.
> **· DPP-4 inhibitors** — weight-neutral, low hypoglycaemia risk, modest efficacy.
> **· Pioglitazone** — fluid retention; **avoid in heart failure.**
> **· Insulin** — when other agents are insufficient, or in acute illness and marked hyperglycaemia.
> `UNVERIFIED — all agents, doses, sequencing and PBS criteria.`

> [!warning] Choose the agent by comorbidity, not just by HbA1c
> **This is the central modern principle.** A patient with heart failure or chronic kidney disease should be on an SGLT2 inhibitor because of the organ protection, **even if their glucose is already at target.** A patient whose main problem is obesity benefits from a GLP-1 agonist. A frail elderly patient at risk of hypoglycaemia should not be on a sulfonylurea.
> **The old approach of adding agents in a fixed sequence to chase a number has been superseded.**

**Ix:** **HbA1c at intervals determined by stability and treatment change** (*why:* monitors control, with the caveats in 0.1; *what:* trend). Blood pressure at every visit (*why:* the intervention with the largest absolute benefit for complications; *what:* control). **Lipids** (*why:* cardiovascular risk management; *what:* profile). **UEC, eGFR and urine albumin-creatinine ratio annually** (*why:* detects nephropathy at the albuminuria stage when it is modifiable; *what:* albuminuria, declining eGFR). **B12 in patients on long-term metformin** (*why:* deficiency causes neuropathy that is otherwise attributed to diabetes itself; *what:* low B12). LFT (*why:* metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease is very common; *what:* transaminitis). **Retinal screening and foot examination at the recommended intervals** (*why:* both detect asymptomatic disease at a treatable stage; *what:* retinopathy, neuropathy, ischaemia). Assessment for sleep apnoea and depression (*why:* both are common, both worsen control, and both are treatable; *what:* symptoms).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Only for hyperglycaemic emergencies — cross-refer [[F0.2]].

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Individualised targets, lifestyle intervention, and pharmacotherapy chosen by comorbidity.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**The annual cycle of care in Australian general practice** — HbA1c, blood pressure, lipids, kidney function and albuminuria, eyes, feet, weight, smoking, physical activity, medication review, immunisation, and psychological wellbeing. **Diabetes educator, dietitian and podiatry referral, and NDSS registration.**

---

## 0.3 Type 1 Diabetes — Management

> [!tip] The regimen
> **Basal-bolus (multiple daily injections)** — a long-acting basal insulin plus rapid-acting boluses with meals, with **carbohydrate counting**, an insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio, and a correction factor.
> **Insulin pumps and hybrid closed-loop systems** — increasingly standard, particularly in younger patients, automating basal delivery in response to continuous glucose data.
> **Continuous or flash glucose monitoring** has largely replaced fingerprick testing for many patients. **"Time in range" is now used alongside HbA1c as a measure of control**, and it captures variability and hypoglycaemia in a way that HbA1c cannot — a patient with a good HbA1c achieved by swinging between hypoglycaemia and hyperglycaemia is not well controlled. **CGM is NDSS-subsidised for eligible groups in Australia.** `UNVERIFIED — eligibility.`

> [!danger] Never omit basal insulin — including when the patient is not eating
> **This is the single most important inpatient point in type 1 diabetes.**
> **A person with type 1 diabetes produces NO endogenous insulin. Basal insulin is required continuously to suppress ketogenesis, regardless of oral intake.**
> **Withholding basal insulin because the patient is nil by mouth, fasting for a procedure, or vomiting causes DKA** — and it happens on wards regularly, because the reasoning "they're not eating so they don't need insulin" is intuitive and wrong.
> **Bolus insulin is adjusted or withheld with meals. Basal insulin continues.** Cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.3.

> [!warning] Sick day management — teach it, and check they remember it
> **· Never stop insulin.** Requirements usually **increase** during illness because of counter-regulatory hormones.
> **· Monitor glucose more frequently, and CHECK KETONES** — every person with type 1 diabetes should have ketone testing available and know when to use it.
> **· Maintain carbohydrate and fluid intake**, using sugary fluids if solid food is not tolerated.
> **· Know when to seek help** — persistent vomiting, rising ketones, inability to keep fluids down, or drowsiness.
> **This education prevents DKA admissions**, and it should be revisited rather than given once at diagnosis.

> [!warning] The psychological dimension, which is substantial and under-addressed
> **Diabetes distress** — the emotional burden of relentless self-management — is common, distinct from depression, and responds to different support.
> **Disordered eating and deliberate insulin omission for weight control ("diabulimia")** occurs, particularly in young women, and causes recurrent DKA and accelerated complications. **Recurrent DKA in a young person should prompt gentle exploration of insulin omission, psychological factors and social circumstances rather than repeated protocol-driven admissions.** Cross-refer [[N8]].
> **Transition from paediatric to adult services is a high-risk period** for disengagement and deterioration, and needs active management rather than a referral letter.

**Ix:** HbA1c and **time in range from CGM data** (*why:* HbA1c alone conceals variability and hypoglycaemia; *what:* both metrics). Ketones during illness or hyperglycaemia (*why:* DKA prevention; *what:* beta-hydroxybutyrate). **Screening for associated autoimmune disease — thyroid function, coeliac serology, and consideration of adrenal insufficiency** (*why:* they cluster with type 1 diabetes, and coeliac disease and Addison disease both cause unexplained hypoglycaemia and poor control; *what:* associated conditions). Complication screening as per 0.5–0.6. **Psychological screening** (*why:* diabetes distress, depression and disordered eating are common and materially affect outcomes; *what:* symptoms).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
DKA management — cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.3.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Basal-bolus or pump therapy with structured education, carbohydrate counting and CGM.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Multidisciplinary care, complication screening, psychological support, and **driving and occupational advice** — cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6.

---

## 0.4 Hypoglycaemia

**D:** Defined by **Whipple triad — symptoms consistent with hypoglycaemia, a low measured plasma glucose, and resolution of symptoms when the glucose is corrected.** `UNVERIFIED — the glucose thresholds used to define and grade hypoglycaemia.`

> [!tip] The symptoms occur in a physiological sequence
> **· Autonomic first (as counter-regulation activates): sweating, tremor, palpitations, hunger, anxiety, pallor.** These are the **warning symptoms.**
> **· Neuroglycopenic second (as the brain runs out of substrate): confusion, difficulty concentrating, slurred speech, behavioural change, drowsiness, seizure, coma, focal neurological deficit mimicking stroke.**
> **Hypoglycaemia can produce a focal deficit and is a stroke mimic — which is why glucose is checked in every suspected stroke.** Cross-refer [[D3]] 0.1.

> [!danger] Hypoglycaemia unawareness — and the fact that it is reversible
> **Recurrent hypoglycaemia blunts the counter-regulatory response, so the autonomic warning symptoms are lost and the first manifestation becomes neuroglycopenia — confusion or collapse without warning.**
> Contributors: **recurrent hypoglycaemia, long diabetes duration, autonomic neuropathy, and beta-blockers** (which mask the adrenergic symptoms, though sweating is preserved).
> **Crucially, it is REVERSIBLE — strict avoidance of hypoglycaemia for a period restores awareness.** This means the correct response to unawareness is to **raise the glycaemic targets deliberately** for weeks to months, not to push harder for control.
> **Hypoglycaemia unawareness has major driving implications** and must be reported — cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6.

> [!warning] Causes in a person with diabetes
> **Too much insulin or sulfonylurea (dose error, wrong insulin, injection into a lipohypertrophic site with erratic absorption) · missed or delayed meal · exercise (which can cause delayed hypoglycaemia many hours later) · ALCOHOL (which inhibits gluconeogenesis, and the hypoglycaemia may be delayed and prolonged and is easily mistaken for intoxication) · weight loss · gastroparesis with mismatched insulin timing · renal or hepatic impairment reducing drug clearance · new adrenal insufficiency or coeliac disease · and drug interactions.**
> **A patient whose insulin requirement suddenly falls has a reason** — weight loss, renal decline, coeliac disease, adrenal insufficiency, or resolving infection.

> [!danger] Hypoglycaemia in a person WITHOUT diabetes always needs explanation
> **· Critical illness — sepsis, liver failure, renal failure, cardiac failure, malnutrition.**
> **· Adrenal insufficiency** — cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.6.
> **· Alcohol**, particularly with poor nutrition.
> **· Drugs** — quinine, quinolones, tramadol, beta-blockers, and **inadvertent or deliberate administration of a sulfonylurea or insulin.**
> **· Insulinoma** — fasting hypoglycaemia with inappropriately raised insulin.
> **· Post-bariatric surgery** — late postprandial hypoglycaemia from exaggerated incretin response.
> **· Non-islet cell tumour hypoglycaemia** (IGF-2 secreting).
> **· Factitious or malicious administration.**

> [!tip] The C-peptide discriminates the cause
> **When investigating hypoglycaemia, take the samples DURING the hypoglycaemic episode — insulin, C-peptide, pro-insulin, beta-hydroxybutyrate and a sulfonylurea screen — before treating.** Afterwards they are uninterpretable.
> **· High insulin with HIGH C-peptide = ENDOGENOUS insulin → insulinoma, OR a sulfonylurea** (which stimulates endogenous secretion) — **hence the sulfonylurea screen, which distinguishes the two and is frequently forgotten.**
> **· High insulin with LOW C-peptide = EXOGENOUS insulin** → therapeutic, factitious or malicious administration. **C-peptide is cleaved from proinsulin in equimolar amounts with endogenous insulin, and is absent from manufactured insulin — which is precisely why it works as the discriminator.**
> **· Low insulin with low C-peptide and raised ketones = an appropriate response** → the cause is not hyperinsulinaemic: think adrenal insufficiency, liver failure, starvation, or critical illness.
> A supervised **72-hour fast** is the formal test for insulinoma.

> [!danger] Sulfonylurea hypoglycaemia is prolonged and recurrent
> **Do not treat a sulfonylurea-induced hypoglycaemia, see the glucose normalise, and discharge the patient.** The drug's effect long outlasts the glucose bolus, and hypoglycaemia recurs — sometimes fatally.
> **These patients require admission, a glucose infusion, prolonged observation, and consideration of octreotide** to suppress the sulfonylurea-driven insulin release.
> This is a recurring and preventable error, particularly with long-acting agents in older patients with renal impairment.

> [!warning] Treatment, and two important caveats
> **· Conscious and able to swallow:** rapid-acting oral carbohydrate, **followed by longer-acting carbohydrate** to prevent recurrence.
> **· Unconscious or unable to swallow:** intravenous glucose, or **intramuscular glucagon.**
> **Glucagon works by mobilising hepatic glycogen — so it FAILS in patients with depleted glycogen stores: those with alcohol-related hypoglycaemia, liver failure, prolonged starvation, or malnutrition.** In those patients, glucose is required.
> **· Give thiamine before or with glucose in the alcohol-dependent or malnourished**, to avoid precipitating Wernicke encephalopathy. Cross-refer [[C2]] 0.7.

**Ix:** **Bedside glucose immediately, and formal laboratory glucose** (*why:* confirms and quantifies; *what:* low glucose). **Insulin, C-peptide, pro-insulin, beta-hydroxybutyrate and sulfonylurea screen — collected DURING the episode** (*why:* the only time they are interpretable, and the C-peptide relationship identifies the mechanism; *what:* the pattern above). **Morning cortisol or short synacthen test** (*why:* adrenal insufficiency causes hypoglycaemia and is frequently missed; *what:* low cortisol). UEC, LFT (*why:* renal and hepatic impairment reduce drug clearance and gluconeogenesis; *what:* organ dysfunction). Coeliac serology and TFTs in a person with type 1 diabetes and new hypoglycaemia (*why:* both reduce insulin requirement and are treatable; *what:* associated disease). Review of the insulin regimen, injection technique and injection sites (*why:* **lipohypertrophy at overused injection sites causes erratic absorption and unpredictable hypoglycaemia — and it is found by examining the sites, which is rarely done**; *what:* lipohypertrophy). Imaging for insulinoma where biochemically confirmed (*why:* localisation for surgery; *what:* pancreatic lesion).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Treat as above. **Take the diagnostic bloods first if the patient is stable and the cause is unexplained.** **Admit sulfonylurea hypoglycaemia.**

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Adjust the regimen, treat the underlying cause, or remove the offending drug.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Restore hypoglycaemia awareness by deliberately relaxing targets.** Education, CGM with alarms, glucagon supply for household members, medical alert identification, and **driving advice** — cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6.

---

## 0.5 Microvascular Complications

> [!tip] Retinopathy
> Progresses from **non-proliferative (microaneurysms, dot and blot haemorrhages, hard exudates, cotton wool spots) to proliferative (neovascularisation, with the risk of vitreous haemorrhage and tractional retinal detachment).**
> **Diabetic MACULOPATHY — macular oedema — is the commonest cause of vision loss in type 2 diabetes**, and it can occur at any stage of retinopathy, including early. **It threatens central vision and is treated with intravitreal anti-VEGF therapy.**
> **Screening is by dilated fundoscopy or retinal photography at defined intervals**, and it is asymptomatic until advanced — **which is the entire justification for screening a patient who reports normal vision.** `UNVERIFIED — screening intervals for type 1, type 2 and pregnancy.`
> **Rapid improvement in glycaemic control can transiently WORSEN retinopathy** — an important and counterintuitive point. It matters when intensifying treatment in someone with longstanding poor control, and in pregnancy, and it is a reason for retinal assessment before and during intensification rather than a reason not to improve control.

> [!tip] Nephropathy
> Progresses from **albuminuria (initially moderately increased, formerly "microalbuminuria") to declining eGFR.**
> **Screen annually with a urine albumin-creatinine ratio AND eGFR** — **either can be abnormal first, and checking only creatinine misses the albuminuric phase when intervention is most effective.**
> **Management: ACE inhibitor or ARB (but not both), blood pressure control, glycaemic control, and — now core — an SGLT2 INHIBITOR, which has substantial renal protective effect independent of glucose lowering.** Non-steroidal mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists have an emerging role. Cross-refer [[H3]].
> **A rise in creatinine after starting an ACE inhibitor or ARB is expected and acceptable within limits — it is not a reason to stop.** Cross-refer [[CV-X]] 0.3.

> [!warning] Neuropathy
> **· Distal symmetrical polyneuropathy** — the commonest, glove-and-stocking, and **the loss of protective sensation is what costs feet.** Cross-refer [[D4]] 0.2.
> **· Autonomic neuropathy** — and it is under-recognised: **gastroparesis** (cross-refer [[C2]] 0.4), **postural hypotension**, erectile dysfunction, bladder dysfunction, gustatory sweating, **hypoglycaemia unawareness**, and **SILENT MYOCARDIAL ISCHAEMIA — which is why a diabetic with unexplained breathlessness, fatigue or vomiting warrants an ECG.**
> **· Mononeuropathies** — cranial nerve palsies, classically a **pupil-sparing third nerve palsy** (cross-refer [[D7]] 0.3), and entrapment neuropathies.
> **· Diabetic amyotrophy** — proximal thigh pain and wasting, often asymmetric, with weight loss.
> **· And remember metformin-associated B12 deficiency as a treatable contributor** — see 0.2.

**Ix:** **Dilated retinal examination or retinal photography at screening intervals** (*why:* asymptomatic and treatable; *what:* retinopathy grade, maculopathy). **Urine ACR and eGFR annually** (*why:* detects nephropathy at the modifiable stage; *what:* albuminuria, eGFR trend). **Monofilament testing and vibration sense** (*why:* identifies loss of protective sensation, which determines foot risk category; *what:* absent protective sensation). **B12** on long-term metformin (*why:* a treatable cause of neuropathy attributed to diabetes; *what:* deficiency). Postural blood pressure (*why:* autonomic neuropathy, and it contributes to falls; *what:* postural drop). ECG (*why:* silent ischaemia; *what:* ischaemic change).

---

## 0.6 Macrovascular Risk and the Diabetic Foot

> [!warning] Cardiovascular disease is the leading cause of death in diabetes
> Management is **the whole cardiovascular risk package, not just glucose**: **blood pressure control, statin therapy, smoking cessation, and antiplatelet therapy where there is established cardiovascular disease.**
> **Glycaemic control has a much larger effect on MICROvascular than on MACROvascular complications** — which is why blood pressure and lipid management deliver more cardiovascular benefit than intensifying glucose control does. Cross-refer [[B2]] 0.1 and [[GER3]].

> [!danger] The diabetic foot — three problems that combine
> **NEUROPATHY (loss of protective sensation) + ISCHAEMIA (peripheral arterial disease) + INFECTION.**
> **The neuropathy is what makes it dangerous, because the injury is PAINLESS** — a stone in the shoe, a new pair of shoes, a burn from a heater, or a nail cut too short goes unnoticed until an ulcer is established.
> **Annual foot examination: sensation (monofilament), pulses, deformity, callus, skin condition, and FOOTWEAR — including looking inside the shoes.**
> **Ulcer management requires offloading, debridement, infection management, revascularisation assessment, and a multidisciplinary foot service.** **Probe-to-bone suggests osteomyelitis.**
> **The strongest predictor of amputation is a previous amputation** — the contralateral limb is at high risk and needs active protection. Cross-refer [[B5]] 0.5–0.6 and [[D4]] 0.2.

> [!danger] Charcot neuroarthropathy — the hot foot that is not cellulitis
> **A hot, swollen, erythematous foot in a person with diabetic neuropathy, often WITHOUT an ulcer and often with little or no pain.**
> **It is routinely misdiagnosed as cellulitis, gout or a sprain**, treated with antibiotics or elevation, and the patient keeps walking on it — **which destroys the architecture of the foot permanently, producing the rocker-bottom deformity and a limb at high risk of ulceration and amputation.**
> **The treatment is IMMEDIATE OFFLOADING and non-weight-bearing, with urgent referral.** **A hot swollen foot in a neuropathic diabetic should be assumed to be Charcot until proven otherwise, and the patient taken off it.**

> [!tip] The other comorbidities worth screening for
> **· Metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD)** — very common, and can progress to fibrosis and cirrhosis. Cross-refer [[C3]] 0.5.
> **· Obstructive sleep apnoea** — common, worsens control, and treatable.
> **· Depression** — twice as common in diabetes, worsens self-management and outcomes.
> **· Erectile dysfunction** — common, distressing, rarely volunteered, and **a marker of vascular disease that should prompt cardiovascular assessment.**
> **· Periodontal disease** — bidirectional relationship with glycaemic control; **dental review is a legitimate part of diabetes care and is almost never mentioned.**
> **· Increased infection risk** — including candidiasis, urinary tract infection, skin and soft tissue infection, and **specific severe infections such as emphysematous pyelonephritis, malignant otitis externa and mucormycosis.**

**Ix:** Blood pressure, lipids, and absolute cardiovascular risk assessment (*why:* these deliver more macrovascular benefit than glucose control; *what:* risk category). **Annual foot assessment with monofilament, pulses and footwear inspection** (*why:* stratifies risk and detects the asymptomatic at-risk foot; *what:* neuropathy, ischaemia, deformity). **Ankle-brachial index, with toe pressures where calcification is likely** (*why:* **medial calcification in diabetes makes the ABI falsely normal or high** — cross-refer [[B5]] 0.5; *what:* perfusion). Imaging for osteomyelitis where an ulcer probes to bone or fails to heal (*why:* changes duration and may require surgery; *what:* bone involvement on MRI). LFT and liver fibrosis assessment (*why:* MASLD; *what:* transaminitis, fibrosis score). Sleep study and depression screening where indicated (*why:* both common and treatable; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index, depressive symptoms).

---

## 0.7 Diabetes in Special Situations

> [!danger] Pregnancy — pre-existing diabetes
> **Preconception planning is the intervention that matters most**, because organogenesis is complete before many women present.
> **· Optimise glycaemic control BEFORE conception** — the risk of congenital malformation relates to periconceptional HbA1c.
> **· High-dose folic acid.**
> **· STOP teratogenic and contraindicated medications — ACE inhibitors, ARBs, statins**, and review other agents.
> **· Retinal assessment before and during pregnancy** — retinopathy can progress rapidly in pregnancy.
> **· Insulin requirements change markedly through pregnancy** and fall abruptly after delivery.

> [!tip] Gestational diabetes
> **Screened for with an oral glucose tolerance test at a defined gestation**, with earlier testing in high-risk women. `UNVERIFIED — timing, thresholds and risk criteria, which differ between guidelines and have been contested.`
> **Risks: macrosomia and shoulder dystocia, neonatal hypoglycaemia, polyhydramnios, pre-eclampsia, and increased caesarean rates.**
> **Management: dietary and lifestyle intervention first, then metformin or insulin.**
> **The point most often missed: a postpartum OGTT is required, and these women carry a substantially increased LIFELONG risk of type 2 diabetes and need ongoing periodic screening and lifestyle intervention.** Gestational diabetes is a window into future risk, and the follow-up is frequently not arranged. Cross-refer [[O2]].

> [!danger] The hospital inpatient — where the errors happen
> **· NEVER stop basal insulin in type 1 diabetes** — see 0.3.
> **· "Sliding scale" correction insulin ALONE is inadequate** — it treats hyperglycaemia after it has occurred rather than preventing it, and it produces oscillation. **Use a basal-bolus regimen with correction doses.**
> **· Steroid-induced hyperglycaemia** — corticosteroids cause a characteristic **predominantly postprandial and afternoon/evening hyperglycaemia**, so morning fasting glucose can look deceptively normal. **Check afternoon or pre-dinner glucose in patients on steroids**, and anticipate it when prescribing them.
> **· Enteral feeding** requires an insulin regimen matched to the feed, and **an interrupted feed with the insulin still running causes hypoglycaemia.**
> **· Hyperglycaemia in hospital is associated with worse outcomes**, but so is hypoglycaemia — the aim is stable moderate control, not tight control.

> [!warning] Perioperative and procedural
> **· Withhold SGLT2 inhibitors before surgery** — because of the risk of **euglycaemic DKA**, which is easily missed perioperatively since the glucose looks acceptable. `UNVERIFIED — the recommended withholding period.`
> **· Metformin and contrast or acute illness** — withhold per local policy.
> **· Consider withholding GLP-1 receptor agonists before procedures** because of delayed gastric emptying and aspiration risk. `UNVERIFIED — this recommendation is recent and evolving.`
> **· Type 1 diabetes fasting for surgery needs basal insulin continued and usually a variable rate insulin infusion**, with first-on-the-list scheduling where possible.

> [!tip] Sick day rules — for both types
> **· Do not stop insulin.** **· Monitor glucose more frequently. · Check ketones (essential in type 1, and in type 2 on an SGLT2 inhibitor). · Maintain hydration and carbohydrate intake. · Withhold the "sick day medications" — metformin, SGLT2 inhibitors, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics and NSAIDs — during acute dehydrating illness, because they precipitate acute kidney injury and, for SGLT2 inhibitors, ketoacidosis. · Know when to seek help.**
> **Every patient with diabetes should have written sick day guidance**, and it should be checked rather than assumed.

> [!tip] Living with diabetes — the practical matters
> **Driving** — glucose checking before driving, carrying carbohydrate, and the reporting obligations, which differ for private and commercial licences and are affected by hypoglycaemia unawareness. Cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6.
> **Employment, insurance, travel** (insulin in hand luggage, time zone adjustment, letters).
> **Religious fasting including Ramadan** — requires planning, risk stratification and regimen adjustment, and patients frequently do not raise it. **Asking about it is better than assuming.**

**Ix:** OGTT for gestational diabetes screening and postpartum reassessment (*why:* both diagnosis and the identification of lifelong risk; *what:* glucose values). Ketones in any unwell patient with diabetes, **including those on SGLT2 inhibitors with normal glucose** (*why:* euglycaemic DKA; *what:* beta-hydroxybutyrate). **Afternoon or pre-dinner glucose in patients on corticosteroids** (*why:* the pattern of steroid hyperglycaemia is missed by fasting testing alone; *what:* postprandial hyperglycaemia). UEC during acute illness (*why:* acute kidney injury from the sick day medications; *what:* rising creatinine).

---

> [!note] Cross-references
Hypoglycaemia as a stroke mimic → [[D3]] 0.5 · Peripheral neuropathy → [[D4]] 0.2 · Gastroparesis → [[C2]] 0.4 · Pupil-sparing third nerve palsy → [[D7]] 0.3 · Peripheral arterial disease and the falsely normal ABI → [[B5]] 0.5–0.6 · Chronic kidney disease → [[H3]] · SGLT2 inhibitors in heart failure → [[CV-X]] 0.3 · Hypertension and absolute risk → [[B2]] 0.1 · MASLD → [[C3]] 0.5 · Adrenal insufficiency → [[F0.3]] 0.6 and [[I4]] · Thyroid disease and autoimmune clustering → [[I1]] · Obesity, weight and lipids → [[I5]] · Deprescribing in the frail elderly → [[GER1]] 0.4 · Pregnancy and gestational diabetes → [[O2]] · Disordered eating and insulin omission → [[N8]] · Driving → [[A10]] 0.6 · Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander health → [[AU1]] · Preventive health and screening → [[GER3]]
