<!-- ===== SOURCE: I5_Weight__Lipids_and_Fluid_Balance.md ===== -->


> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Physiology, diagnostic reasoning and discriminators are reliable at intern level. **Every BMI category, lipid target, correction rate, dose and threshold carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.**
> **All sodium correction rate limits in 0.5 are omitted, not flagged** — over-rapid correction causes irreversible osmotic demyelination, and this is not a figure to reproduce from memory. Obtain it from eTG and your health network's protocol. Verify lipid management against the **Australian absolute cardiovascular risk calculator** and PBS criteria, and obesity pharmacotherapy against current PBS and TGA status, which is changing rapidly.
>
> **Acid-base, DKA, isotonic dehydration and third-spacing are built in [[F0.2]]** and are cross-referenced rather than repeated.

---

## 0.1 Obesity

> [!warning] BMI is a population tool with real individual limitations
> **It does not distinguish fat from muscle**, so it misclassifies muscular individuals, and it **underestimates adiposity in older people** who have lost muscle.
> **Cut-offs differ by ethnicity: the thresholds for increased metabolic risk are LOWER in South and East Asian populations**, which matters in Australia's population — a person of South Asian background can have substantial metabolic risk at a BMI considered normal by European criteria. `UNVERIFIED — the ethnicity-specific thresholds.`
> **Central adiposity predicts metabolic and cardiovascular risk better than BMI**, so **waist circumference adds information that BMI does not** and is worth measuring.

> [!danger] Weight stigma is a clinical problem, not a courtesy issue
> **People with obesity report avoiding or delaying healthcare because of previous experiences of judgement**, and symptoms are attributed to weight without assessment — which is how serious diagnoses get missed in larger patients.
> **Practical points that change the encounter:**
> **· Ask permission before discussing weight** — "would it be alright if we talked about weight today?" — and accept a no.
> **· Use person-first, neutral language** — "a person with obesity" rather than "an obese person", "weight" rather than "fatness".
> **· Do not attribute a symptom to weight without evaluating it** as you would in anyone else.
> **· Ensure the practical environment is appropriate** — correctly sized blood pressure cuffs, gowns, chairs and equipment. Their absence communicates something.
> **This is directly examinable in an OSCE and it materially affects whether the person returns.**

> [!tip] It is not simply energy balance — look for contributors
> **· Drugs — a genuinely major and reversible contributor.** **Antipsychotics (olanzapine and clozapine most), sodium valproate, mirtazapine, tricyclics, lithium, insulin and sulfonylureas, corticosteroids, gabapentinoids, and some beta-blockers.** **Review the drug chart before intensifying lifestyle advice.**
> **· Endocrine** — hypothyroidism, Cushing syndrome, PCOS, hypothalamic damage.
> **· Obstructive sleep apnoea and sleep deprivation**, which are both consequence and cause.
> **· Reduced mobility and chronic pain.**
> **· Social determinants** — food cost and availability, housing, shift work, and time.
> **· Psychological** — depression, trauma, binge eating disorder. Cross-refer [[N8]].

> [!info] Management, and what the evidence supports
> **· Lifestyle** — **adherence matters more than dietary composition**; any sustainable energy deficit works, and the best diet is the one the person can maintain. **Physical activity has health benefits independent of weight loss** and is essential for maintaining loss, though it is a weak tool for producing it.
> **· Realistic goals** — **5 to 10% weight loss produces meaningful improvement in blood pressure, glycaemia, lipids and sleep apnoea**, and framing that as the target is more useful and more achievable than a "normal BMI".
> **· Pharmacotherapy** — **GLP-1 receptor agonists and dual agonists have substantially changed what is achievable medically.** Also orlistat, phentermine, and naltrexone-bupropion.
> **The essential counselling point: weight is regained when the medication stops.** These are treatments for a chronic condition, not a course. Availability, cost and PBS status are significant practical barriers in Australia. `UNVERIFIED — agents, doses, and current PBS and supply status, which is changing.`
> **· Bariatric and metabolic surgery** — the most effective durable intervention, with **remission of type 2 diabetes in a substantial proportion.** `UNVERIFIED — BMI and comorbidity eligibility thresholds.`

> [!danger] Post-bariatric surgery — two things every doctor should know
> **1. LIFELONG nutritional supplementation and monitoring is required** — **vitamin B12, iron, folate, thiamine, calcium and vitamin D, and copper and zinc.** **Deficiencies present years later as anaemia, neuropathy, osteomalacia, and — in the case of thiamine — Wernicke encephalopathy, which occurs after bariatric surgery particularly with vomiting.** Cross-refer [[C2]] 0.7 and [[D2]] 0.5.
> **2. ABDOMINAL PAIN AFTER GASTRIC BYPASS IS AN INTERNAL HERNIA UNTIL PROVEN OTHERWISE.** It can cause closed-loop obstruction and bowel infarction, **CT is frequently normal or equivocal**, and the correct response to significant unexplained abdominal pain in a bypass patient is **urgent surgical review by the bariatric team, not observation.** This is a genuine cause of preventable death and is missed by clinicians unfamiliar with the anatomy.
> Also: **dumping syndrome, post-bariatric hypoglycaemia (late postprandial), increased alcohol sensitivity and risk of alcohol use disorder, and a recommendation to delay pregnancy for a period after surgery** because of the rapid weight loss and nutritional risk.

**S/Smx:** Assess the weight history, previous attempts, eating pattern, physical activity, sleep, mood, medications, and the complications below.

**Ix:** **HbA1c or fasting glucose, lipids, LFT, blood pressure** (*why:* the metabolic complications, several of which are asymptomatic; *what:* diabetes, dyslipidaemia, MASLD, hypertension). TFTs (*why:* hypothyroidism as a contributor, though it rarely explains substantial obesity; *what:* raised TSH). **Sleep study where symptoms suggest obstructive sleep apnoea** (*why:* very common, treatable, and it worsens metabolic control and cardiovascular risk; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index). Liver fibrosis assessment where MASLD is present (*why:* fibrosis stage determines prognosis; *what:* elastography or fibrosis score — cross-refer [[C3]] 0.5). Screening for Cushing syndrome **only where there are discriminating features** (*why:* untargeted testing generates false positives, and obesity itself is a pseudo-Cushing state; *what:* proximal myopathy, thin skin, bruising, wide purple striae — cross-refer [[I4]] 0.4).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Multicomponent lifestyle intervention, review of obesogenic medications, pharmacotherapy, and surgical referral where indicated.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Treat obesity as a chronic relapsing condition requiring ongoing management, not a one-off intervention.** Lifelong nutritional monitoring after surgery. Address the complications independently.

---

## 0.2 Unintentional Weight Loss

> [!danger] Unintentional weight loss is always significant
> `UNVERIFIED — the percentage and timeframe that define clinically significant loss.`
> **First, confirm it is real** — documented weights, or a change in clothing or belt size, rather than an impression. **Self-reported weight loss without corroboration is unreliable in both directions.**

> [!tip] The single most useful discriminating question: what has happened to the appetite?
> **· Weight loss with REDUCED appetite** — malignancy, chronic infection, chronic organ failure, depression, medications, dementia, social factors.
> **· Weight loss with PRESERVED OR INCREASED appetite** — **a much shorter list: thyrotoxicosis, uncontrolled diabetes, malabsorption (coeliac disease, pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, inflammatory bowel disease), and phaeochromocytoma.**
> That one question narrows the differential immediately, and it is frequently not asked.

> [!warning] The differential
> **· Malignancy** — accounts for a substantial proportion, and may be occult.
> **· Gastrointestinal** — coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, chronic pancreatitis, peptic ulcer disease, dysphagia, chronic mesenteric ischaemia (**postprandial pain and food fear**).
> **· Endocrine** — thyrotoxicosis (**including apathetic thyrotoxicosis in the elderly** — cross-refer [[I1]] 0.3), diabetes, adrenal insufficiency, phaeochromocytoma.
> **· Infection** — tuberculosis, HIV, infective endocarditis, chronic abscess. Cross-refer [[K1]] 0.3.
> **· Psychiatric — DEPRESSION is a leading and treatable cause, particularly in older people**, alongside eating disorders and substance use.
> **· Chronic organ failure** — cardiac cachexia, COPD, chronic kidney disease, cirrhosis.
> **· Neurological** — dementia, Parkinson disease, motor neurone disease, stroke — largely through **dysphagia and reduced intake.**
> **· Medications** — a long list, and worth reviewing.
> **· SOCIAL AND ORAL** — **poverty, isolation, bereavement, inability to shop or cook, and poor dentition or ill-fitting dentures.** **These are common, correctable and consistently under-considered**, particularly in older people. Cross-refer [[GER2]] 0.3.

**Ix:** **Targeted history and full examination first, including the mouth and dentition, and a mood assessment** (*why:* a large proportion of causes are identified clinically, and untargeted imaging in a patient with an obvious social or psychiatric cause is unhelpful; *what:* the likely cause). **FBC, ESR and CRP, UEC, LFT, calcium, glucose or HbA1c, TFTs, and coeliac serology** (*why:* the baseline that covers the metabolic, inflammatory, endocrine and malabsorptive causes; *what:* the abnormality). **HIV testing** (*why:* under-offered, treatable, and a recognised cause; *what:* positive). CXR and urinalysis (*why:* malignancy, tuberculosis, infection; *what:* findings). **Age-appropriate cancer screening brought up to date, and faecal occult blood or direct colonic investigation where indicated** (*why:* occult gastrointestinal malignancy; *what:* neoplasia — cross-refer [[C4]] 0.5). **Directed imaging based on findings rather than a whole-body scan** (*why:* untargeted imaging has low yield and a high incidentaloma rate; *what:* the suspected lesion).

> [!tip] When the workup is negative
> **A meaningful proportion of unintentional weight loss never receives a diagnosis**, and a negative initial evaluation in a patient without red flags is reasonably reassuring.
> **The correct response is structured follow-up with repeat weighing and reassessment over months, rather than escalating imaging** — a cancer that was occult at presentation usually declares itself within that period, and repeated scanning in the interim finds incidentalomas rather than answers.

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Rarely required.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Nutritional support and dietitian involvement alongside investigating the cause** — the malnutrition itself worsens outcomes and should be treated in parallel, not after the diagnosis. Cross-refer [[GER2]] 0.3.

---

## 0.3 Lipid Disorders

**A/P:** **LDL cholesterol is causally related to atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease** — this is established by genetic, epidemiological and randomised evidence, and it underpins the entire treatment strategy: **lowering LDL reduces events, and the benefit is proportional to the absolute reduction achieved.**
**In hypertriglyceridaemia and diabetes, LDL underestimates risk**, and **non-HDL cholesterol or apolipoprotein B** better capture the total atherogenic particle burden.

> [!danger] Familial hypercholesterolaemia — common, dangerous, and massively underdiagnosed
> **An autosomal dominant disorder of LDL receptor function, affecting roughly 1 in 250 people — making it one of the commonest serious inherited conditions — and the great majority of affected Australians are undiagnosed.**
> **Untreated, it causes markedly elevated LDL from birth and premature coronary disease**, sometimes in the fourth or fifth decade.
> **Clues: a very high LDL · a personal or family history of PREMATURE cardiovascular disease (under about 55 in men, 60 in women) · TENDON XANTHOMATA (Achilles, extensor tendons of the hands — feel for them, they are specific) · corneal arcus under about 45 · and xanthelasma.**
> **Diagnosis uses clinical criteria such as the Dutch Lipid Clinic Network score, with genetic testing available.** `UNVERIFIED — the criteria and LDL thresholds.`
> **CASCADE SCREENING OF FIRST-DEGREE RELATIVES IS THE HIGHEST-VALUE ACTION** — each first-degree relative has a one-in-two chance of being affected, and identifying them young allows treatment before disease develops. **Diagnosing one index case should generate a family.**

> [!warning] Always exclude secondary causes before labelling a primary lipid disorder
> **· HYPOTHYROIDISM — check the TSH in every dyslipidaemia. It is common, it causes marked hypercholesterolaemia, it resolves with thyroxine, and treating the lipids without treating the thyroid is both futile and increases statin myopathy risk.**
> **· Diabetes and insulin resistance** — typically a raised triglyceride with low HDL.
> **· Nephrotic syndrome** — marked hypercholesterolaemia.
> **· Chronic kidney disease · cholestatic liver disease · alcohol (particularly raising triglycerides) · obesity · pregnancy.**
> **· Drugs — thiazides, corticosteroids, antipsychotics, retinoids (isotretinoin markedly raises triglycerides), immunosuppressants, oestrogens, and some antiretrovirals.**

> [!danger] Very high triglycerides — the priority is preventing pancreatitis, not cardiovascular disease
> Above a threshold, hypertriglyceridaemia causes **acute pancreatitis**, and that becomes the immediate concern.
> **Management: alcohol abstinence, strict dietary fat restriction, glycaemic control, fibrates and omega-3 fatty acids, and cessation of contributing drugs.** Cross-refer [[C7]] 0.1.
> **And remember the assay trap: in hypertriglyceridaemia-induced pancreatitis, the AMYLASE may be falsely NORMAL** because the lipaemic serum interferes — so measure lipase and triglycerides. `UNVERIFIED — the triglyceride threshold at which pancreatitis risk becomes significant.`

> [!tip] Treatment — driven by absolute risk, not by the number alone
> Australian practice uses **absolute cardiovascular risk assessment** rather than treating a lipid threshold in isolation — cross-refer [[B2]] 0.1 and [[GER3]].
> **Exceptions treated regardless of calculated risk: established cardiovascular disease, familial hypercholesterolaemia, diabetes with certain features, chronic kidney disease, and very high individual risk factor levels.**
> **· Statins — first-line**, with high-intensity therapy for established disease.
> **· Ezetimibe** — added where statin alone is insufficient or not tolerated.
> **· PCSK9 inhibitors and inclisiran** — for familial hypercholesterolaemia and very high risk, with restricted access.
> **· Fibrates and omega-3** — for hypertriglyceridaemia.
> `UNVERIFIED — agents, intensities, targets and PBS authority criteria.`

> [!warning] Statin-associated muscle symptoms — mostly not caused by the statin
> **Muscle symptoms are reported commonly, but blinded rechallenge studies show that the majority are NOT attributable to the drug — a substantial nocebo effect.**
> **This matters because statin discontinuation after a myocardial infarction increases mortality**, and patients are frequently taken off them permanently on the basis of aches that were not caused by them.
> **A reasonable approach: check CK and TSH, stop temporarily to see if symptoms resolve, then REchallenge — with the same statin, a lower dose, an alternate-day regimen, or a different statin.** Most patients tolerate something.
> **Genuine myopathy with a markedly raised CK, and rhabdomyolysis, are rare but real** — and **statin-associated immune-mediated necrotising myopathy CONTINUES to progress after the drug is stopped and requires immunosuppression.** Cross-refer [[D4]] 0.6.
> **Check the TSH** — untreated hypothyroidism substantially increases statin myopathy risk.

**Ix:** **Fasting or non-fasting lipid profile** (*why:* non-fasting is acceptable for screening in most circumstances, which improves uptake; *what:* total cholesterol, LDL, HDL, triglycerides, non-HDL). **TSH, glucose or HbA1c, UEC, LFT, and urine protein** (*why:* the secondary causes, all of which change management; *what:* hypothyroidism, diabetes, renal disease, cholestasis). **Absolute cardiovascular risk calculation** (*why:* it, not the cholesterol number, determines treatment in primary prevention; *what:* risk category). **Family history of premature cardiovascular disease, and examination for tendon xanthomata and corneal arcus** (*why:* identifies familial hypercholesterolaemia, which is otherwise missed; *what:* the clinical criteria). Lipoprotein(a) in selected patients (*why:* an independent genetically determined risk factor, measured once, that refines risk in those with premature or familial disease; *what:* elevation). CK where muscle symptoms (*why:* distinguishes true myopathy from myalgia; *what:* elevation).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Only for hypertriglyceridaemic pancreatitis.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Lifestyle plus lipid-lowering therapy determined by absolute risk, with treatment of secondary causes first.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Adherence support — persistence with statins is poor and it is the main determinant of benefit.** **Cascade screening of relatives in familial hypercholesterolaemia.**

---

## 0.4 Metabolic Syndrome and Integrated Risk

> [!tip] The clustering, and a caveat about its usefulness
> **Central obesity plus a combination of raised triglycerides, low HDL cholesterol, raised blood pressure and raised fasting glucose**, unified by **insulin resistance.** `UNVERIFIED — the diagnostic criteria, which differ between definitions.`
> **The clustering is real and predicts type 2 diabetes and cardiovascular disease — but the label itself adds limited value**, because **each component requires treatment on its own merits regardless of whether the patient meets the criteria.** A patient with hypertension and dyslipidaemia is treated for both whether or not they qualify as having "metabolic syndrome".
> **Its clinical utility is as a prompt: finding one component should trigger assessment for the others**, and it identifies a phenotype that responds particularly well to weight loss and physical activity.

> [!warning] Look for the associated conditions that are not in the definition
> **· Metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD)** — extremely common in this group, and can progress to fibrosis and cirrhosis. Cross-refer [[C3]] 0.5.
> **· Obstructive sleep apnoea** — common, bidirectional, and treatable.
> **· Polycystic ovary syndrome** — cross-refer [[I4]] 0.6.
> **· Hyperuricaemia and gout.**
> **· Chronic kidney disease.**

**Ix:** Waist circumference, blood pressure, fasting lipids and glucose or HbA1c (*why:* the components; *what:* the cluster). LFT and liver fibrosis assessment (*why:* MASLD; *what:* steatosis, fibrosis). Sleep study where indicated (*why:* OSA; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index). **Absolute cardiovascular risk calculation** (*why:* integrates the components into a treatment decision; *what:* risk category).

### 0.4.1–0.4.3 Mx
As per the individual components, with weight loss and physical activity addressing several simultaneously.

---

## 0.5 Sodium and Water Balance

> [!danger] Hyponatraemia is a water problem, not a salt problem
> **Serum sodium reflects the ratio of sodium to water, not the total body sodium.** A patient can be hyponatraemic while total body sodium is high (heart failure), normal (SIADH) or low (diuretics, vomiting).
> **This is why the treatment is determined by volume status and by the cause, not by "giving salt".**

> [!tip] The four-step approach
> **1. Is it TRUE hyponatraemia?**
> **· Pseudohyponatraemia** — a laboratory artefact from **severe hyperlipidaemia or paraproteinaemia** displacing plasma water. **Serum osmolality is NORMAL.**
> **· Hypertonic (translocational) hyponatraemia** — **hyperglycaemia** or mannitol drawing water out of cells. **Serum osmolality is HIGH.** **In marked hyperglycaemia, the sodium must be corrected for the glucose before it is interpreted**, and it will rise as the glucose is treated.
> **2. Measure SERUM OSMOLALITY** — this distinguishes the above from true hypotonic hyponatraemia.
> **3. Assess VOLUME STATUS clinically** — hypovolaemic, euvolaemic or hypervolaemic. This is the step that is done poorly and it drives the whole diagnosis.
> **4. Measure URINE SODIUM and URINE OSMOLALITY** — which refine what the clinical assessment cannot.

> [!info] The differential by volume status
> **HYPOVOLAEMIC** — total body water and sodium both reduced, sodium more so.
> **· Renal losses (urine sodium HIGH): diuretics — especially THIAZIDES, which are the commonest drug cause · adrenal insufficiency · cerebral salt wasting · osmotic diuresis.**
> **· Extrarenal losses (urine sodium LOW, as the kidney appropriately conserves): vomiting, diarrhoea, burns, third-spacing, pancreatitis.**
> **EUVOLAEMIC**
> **· SIADH — the commonest, and a diagnosis of exclusion requiring euvolaemia, low serum osmolality, INAPPROPRIATELY CONCENTRATED urine, urine sodium not low, and normal thyroid and adrenal function.** Causes: **malignancy (classically small cell lung carcinoma) · CNS disease · pulmonary disease · drugs (SSRIs, carbamazepine, antipsychotics, PPIs, chemotherapy) · pain, nausea and the post-operative state**, which are extremely common and often overlooked.
> **· Hypothyroidism and secondary adrenal insufficiency** — **which is why TSH and cortisol are part of the hyponatraemia workup, not an afterthought.**
> **· Primary polydipsia and low solute intake ("beer potomania", "tea and toast")** — where urine osmolality is LOW because the kidney is appropriately excreting water.
> **HYPERVOLAEMIC** — total body water increased more than sodium.
> **· Heart failure, cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome, advanced chronic kidney disease.** Urine sodium is low in the first three (avid retention) and higher in renal failure.

> [!danger] The correction rate is the dangerous part, and it cuts both ways
> **· ACUTE hyponatraemia (developing over less than about 48 hours) causes CEREBRAL OEDEMA** — headache, vomiting, confusion, seizures, respiratory arrest — because the brain has not had time to adapt. **This is the immediate threat.**
> **· CHRONIC hyponatraemia is better tolerated because the brain extrudes osmolytes to adapt — but that adaptation is exactly why RAPID CORRECTION causes OSMOTIC DEMYELINATION SYNDROME**, with delayed, often irreversible, and sometimes devastating neurological injury (quadriparesis, dysarthria, dysphagia, locked-in syndrome) appearing days after the sodium was "successfully" corrected.
> **`UNVERIFIED — the maximum permitted rate of sodium rise per 24 hours, and the more conservative limit in high-risk patients, are DELIBERATELY OMITTED from this file. Obtain them from eTG and your health network's protocol. This is the single figure in electrolyte medicine least suitable for recall.`**
> **Risk factors for osmotic demyelination: chronicity, sodium very low at presentation, alcohol use disorder, malnutrition, liver disease, hypokalaemia, and advanced age.**
> **Severe symptoms — seizures or coma — warrant hypertonic saline regardless of chronicity**, because cerebral oedema will kill sooner than demyelination will. But the total rise over 24 hours must still be limited.
> **Watch for AUTOCORRECTION.** When the cause is removed — desmopressin stopped, hypovolaemia corrected, cortisol replaced — **ADH switches off and a large water diuresis follows, overshooting the target rapidly.** Monitor the sodium frequently, and be prepared to give free water or desmopressin to slow it down.

> [!warning] Hypernatraemia is nearly always a problem of ACCESS TO WATER
> **Hypernatraemia means a water deficit.** In a conscious person with an intact thirst mechanism and access to fluid, it is almost impossible to become hypernatraemic — the thirst is overwhelming.
> **So hypernatraemia essentially always means: an infant, an older or cognitively impaired person who cannot ask for or reach a drink, an intubated or unconscious patient, or someone with diabetes insipidus or an impaired thirst mechanism.**
> **In an aged care or hospital setting, hypernatraemia is frequently a marker of inadequate care** — a person who was not offered enough to drink — and it should prompt that reflection rather than only a fluid prescription.
> **Correct slowly** — rapid correction of chronic hypernatraemia causes cerebral oedema. `UNVERIFIED — the correction rate.`

**Ix:** **Serum osmolality, urine osmolality and urine sodium — taken together and BEFORE giving fluids** (*why:* fluid administration changes all three and makes the results uninterpretable, which is why the samples must be collected at presentation; *what:* the pattern that classifies the hyponatraemia). **Clinical volume assessment** (*why:* it determines the entire management; *what:* hypo-, eu- or hypervolaemia). **TSH and morning cortisol** (*why:* hypothyroidism and adrenal insufficiency both cause hyponatraemia, both are treatable, and both are missed if not specifically sought — **and SIADH cannot be diagnosed until they are excluded**; *what:* abnormal results). Glucose and lipids (*why:* the causes of pseudo- and hypertonic hyponatraemia; *what:* hyperglycaemia, lipaemia). **Full medication review** (*why:* thiazides, SSRIs, carbamazepine, PPIs and antipsychotics are among the commonest causes and are reversible; *what:* culprit drug). CXR and imaging where SIADH is confirmed and unexplained (*why:* **small cell lung carcinoma and CNS pathology are important causes of SIADH**; *what:* malignancy). **Frequent repeat sodium during correction** (*why:* both under- and over-correction cause harm, and autocorrection can be rapid; *what:* rate of rise).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
**Hypertonic saline for seizures or coma.** Otherwise, treat the cause and the volume state — **fluid restriction in SIADH, saline in hypovolaemia, diuresis and treatment of the underlying disease in hypervolaemia.** **Monitor the sodium frequently.**

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Stop the offending drug. Treat the malignancy or endocrine cause.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Review of diuretics and other contributing drugs, and — in recurrent hyponatraemia — fluid advice and monitoring.

---

## 0.6 Potassium Disorders

> [!danger] Hyperkalaemia — treat the ECG, not the number
> **Causes: renal impairment (the commonest) · DRUGS — ACE inhibitors and ARBs, mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists, NSAIDs, TRIMETHOPRIM, heparin, beta-blockers, digoxin, calcineurin inhibitors · acidosis · tissue breakdown (rhabdomyolysis, tumour lysis, haemolysis, burns) · adrenal insufficiency · stored blood transfusion.**
> **The drug combination that causes it repeatedly: an ACE inhibitor or ARB plus a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist plus an NSAID, in a patient with declining renal function** — and trimethoprim added for a urinary infection is a classic tipping point.

> [!warning] Pseudohyperkalaemia — exclude it in a well patient with a normal ECG
> **A haemolysed sample · prolonged tourniquet time or fist clenching · delayed sample processing · marked thrombocytosis or leucocytosis.**
> **In a well patient with no ECG changes and no explanation, repeat the sample properly before treating.**
> **But if there are ECG changes, treat immediately and repeat afterwards** — the risk of waiting exceeds the risk of treating a spurious result.

> [!tip] The ECG progression
> **Peaked (tall, narrow, symmetrical) T waves → flattening and loss of P waves with PR prolongation → widening QRS → sine wave → asystole or ventricular fibrillation.**
> **The ECG changes correlate poorly with the absolute level and better with the RATE of rise**, so a chronically dialysed patient may tolerate a level that would arrest someone else. **The ECG determines urgency, not the number.**

> [!info] Treatment — three separate jobs, and calcium does not do the second or third
> **1. STABILISE the myocardium — intravenous CALCIUM (gluconate or chloride).** **It does NOT lower the potassium at all** — it raises the threshold potential and buys time. **Its effect is short-lived and it may need repeating.** Give it whenever there are ECG changes.
> **2. SHIFT potassium into cells — INSULIN with DEXTROSE**, and **nebulised salbutamol** as an adjunct. **Monitor for rebound hypoglycaemia after insulin-dextrose, which is common and is a recognised iatrogenic harm — check the glucose afterwards.** Sodium bicarbonate has a limited role, mainly in acidosis.
> **3. REMOVE potassium from the body — the only step that actually lowers total body potassium.** Potassium binders, diuretics if the patient is fluid-replete with adequate renal function, and **dialysis**, which is definitive.
> **The error to avoid: giving calcium and insulin-dextrose, seeing the potassium fall, and stopping there. The potassium is still in the body and will re-emerge.** Removal, or correction of the cause, is required.
> `UNVERIFIED — all agents, doses and the potassium thresholds triggering each step.`

> [!danger] Hypokalaemia — and the reason it will not correct
> **Causes: gastrointestinal loss (vomiting, diarrhoea, high-output stoma, laxative misuse) · renal loss (DIURETICS, hyperaldosteronism, Cushing syndrome, renal tubular acidosis, Bartter and Gitelman syndromes, amphotericin) · intracellular SHIFT (insulin, beta-agonists including salbutamol nebulisers, ALKALOSIS, refeeding, periodic paralysis) · and poor intake.**
> **CHECK THE MAGNESIUM. Refractory hypokalaemia is hypomagnesaemia until proven otherwise** — magnesium depletion causes renal potassium wasting that cannot be overcome by potassium replacement alone. Cross-refer [[I3]] 0.6.
> **ECG: flattened T waves, ST depression, prominent U waves, and a prolonged QT with the risk of TORSADES DE POINTES** — cross-refer [[B3]] 0.3. **Hypokalaemia also potentiates digoxin toxicity.**
> **Replacement: oral where possible.** Intravenous potassium has **strict rate limits, requires cardiac monitoring above a certain concentration, and concentrated solutions must be given centrally** — **rapid intravenous potassium causes cardiac arrest, and it is a recognised cause of inpatient death.** `UNVERIFIED — all concentrations, rates and monitoring requirements.`

**Ix:** **Repeat sample with proper technique in an unexplained abnormal result in a well patient** (*why:* pseudohyperkalaemia is common; *what:* true value). **ECG immediately in hyperkalaemia** (*why:* it determines urgency and is faster than the laboratory; *what:* peaked T waves, QRS widening). UEC and eGFR (*why:* renal impairment is the commonest cause and determines management; *what:* renal function). **MAGNESIUM** (*why:* refractory hypokalaemia; *what:* low magnesium). VBG (*why:* acid-base disturbance drives transcellular shift, and gives a rapid potassium; *what:* pH, potassium). CK (*why:* rhabdomyolysis; *what:* elevation). **Full medication review** (*why:* drugs are the commonest reversible cause in both directions; *what:* culprit). **Morning cortisol and aldosterone-renin ratio where unexplained** (*why:* adrenal insufficiency causes hyperkalaemia and primary aldosteronism causes hypokalaemia, and both are diagnosable and treatable; *what:* the endocrine cause — cross-refer [[I4]] 0.3, 0.5). **Urine potassium** where the cause is unclear (*why:* **distinguishes renal wasting from gastrointestinal loss or shift**, which have entirely different differentials; *what:* fractional excretion).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
**Hyperkalaemia with ECG changes: calcium, then insulin-dextrose and salbutamol, then removal.** Check glucose after insulin. **Hypokalaemia with arrhythmia: replace with cardiac monitoring, and correct magnesium.**

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the cause and remove the offending drugs.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Review the combination of renin-angiotensin blockade, mineralocorticoid antagonists, NSAIDs and trimethoprim in patients with renal impairment**, and monitor potassium after any change. Dietary advice where recurrent.

> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Physiology, interpretation and clinical reasoning are reliable at intern level. **Every reference range, threshold, dose, infusion rate and surgical criterion carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify against **eTG Endocrinology**, the **Australian Medicines Handbook**, and — for vitamin D and bone health — **Healthy Bones Australia** and current endocrine society guidance.
>
> **Osteoporosis is built in [[GER1]] 0.6** and is cross-referenced rather than repeated.

---

## 0.1 Calcium Homeostasis and Interpreting the Result

**A/P:** Serum calcium is defended within a narrow range by three hormones acting on three organs.
**Parathyroid hormone** rises when ionised calcium falls, and it: **mobilises calcium from bone · increases renal calcium reabsorption while INCREASING phosphate EXCRETION · and activates renal 1-alpha-hydroxylase**, converting 25-hydroxyvitamin D to active **calcitriol**, which increases gut absorption of both calcium and phosphate.
**The reciprocal relationship between calcium and phosphate under PTH is the key to interpreting the biochemistry: PTH raises calcium and LOWERS phosphate.** So **high calcium with LOW phosphate suggests PTH excess**, whereas **high calcium with HIGH phosphate suggests a non-PTH mechanism** — malignancy, vitamin D excess, or renal failure.

> [!tip] Correct for albumin — but ionised calcium is what actually matters
> **Roughly half of circulating calcium is bound to albumin, and only the ionised fraction is physiologically active.**
> **A low albumin gives a low TOTAL calcium with a normal ionised calcium** — hence the correction formula, which is an approximation. `UNVERIFIED — the correction formula.`
> **Ionised calcium should be measured directly where accuracy matters** — critical illness, massive transfusion, dialysis, and where the corrected value does not fit the clinical picture.

> [!danger] Alkalosis causes symptomatic hypocalcaemia with a normal total calcium
> **Alkalosis increases the binding of calcium to albumin, reducing the ionised fraction.**
> **This is why hyperventilation causes perioral tingling and carpopedal spasm despite a completely normal total calcium** — and why a patient with a metabolic alkalosis from vomiting can develop tetany. Cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.7.
> **Correcting the alkalosis corrects the calcium.** Giving calcium to a hyperventilating patient treats the wrong problem.

> [!warning] The single most useful investigation in any calcium abnormality is the PTH
> **PTH is the branch point of the entire differential.**
> **· High or inappropriately NORMAL PTH with a high calcium = PTH-driven.** A "normal" PTH in the face of hypercalcaemia is inappropriate — it should be suppressed.
> **· Suppressed PTH with a high calcium = non-PTH-driven** — malignancy until proven otherwise.
> **· Low PTH with a low calcium = hypoparathyroidism.**
> **· High PTH with a low calcium = an appropriate response** — vitamin D deficiency, chronic kidney disease, or PTH resistance.
> **Send calcium, phosphate, albumin, PTH, magnesium, renal function, ALP and 25-hydroxyvitamin D together** — sending calcium alone and then chasing the rest sequentially wastes days.

---

## 0.2 Hypercalcaemia

> [!tip] The two-branch differential, decided by the PTH
> **PTH-MEDIATED (PTH high or inappropriately normal):**
> **· Primary hyperparathyroidism — the commonest cause in the community and in outpatients.**
> **· Tertiary hyperparathyroidism** — autonomous glands after prolonged secondary stimulation, typically in chronic kidney disease.
> **· Familial hypocalciuric hypercalcaemia (FHH)** — see the danger box below.
> **· Lithium** — shifts the calcium set point.
> **NON-PTH-MEDIATED (PTH suppressed):**
> **· MALIGNANCY — the commonest cause in HOSPITAL inpatients**, by three mechanisms: **PTH-related peptide (humoral hypercalcaemia, classically squamous cell carcinoma, renal and breast) · direct osteolytic metastases (breast, myeloma) · and tumour production of calcitriol (lymphoma).**
> **· Granulomatous disease — sarcoidosis and tuberculosis** — where macrophages express 1-alpha-hydroxylase and produce calcitriol autonomously.
> **· Vitamin D toxicity**, including from supplements.
> **· Thyrotoxicosis · immobilisation · adrenal insufficiency · phaeochromocytoma · thiazide diuretics · vitamin A excess · milk-alkali syndrome (now usually calcium supplement excess).**

> [!danger] Familial hypocalciuric hypercalcaemia — the diagnosis that prevents a pointless operation
> **An autosomal dominant, benign, lifelong condition caused by an inactivating calcium-sensing receptor mutation.** The set point is reset upward: calcium is mildly high, PTH is normal or mildly raised — **biochemically it looks exactly like mild primary hyperparathyroidism.**
> **The discriminator is the URINARY CALCIUM: it is LOW in FHH (the kidney avidly reabsorbs calcium) and normal or high in primary hyperparathyroidism.** A **24-hour urinary calcium or calcium-creatinine clearance ratio** distinguishes them. `UNVERIFIED — the ratio and its threshold.`
> **Why it matters: FHH requires NO treatment and NO surgery. Parathyroidectomy does not cure it** — and patients have undergone unnecessary neck exploration, sometimes repeatedly, because the urinary calcium was never measured.
> **Clues: mild hypercalcaemia present for years, from a young age, asymptomatic, with a family history and no complications.** **Measure urinary calcium before referring anyone for parathyroid surgery.**

> [!warning] Symptoms — "stones, bones, abdominal groans and psychic moans"
> **· Renal — polyuria and polydipsia (hypercalcaemia causes a nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, which is why these patients are VOLUME DEPLETED), nephrolithiasis, nephrocalcinosis, renal impairment.**
> **· Bone — pain, fractures, osteoporosis.**
> **· Gastrointestinal — anorexia, nausea, vomiting, constipation, and pancreatitis and peptic ulceration in severe cases.**
> **· Neuropsychiatric — fatigue, weakness, depression, confusion, and in severe hypercalcaemia drowsiness, coma.**
> **· Cardiac — SHORT QT interval, and arrhythmia and hypertension.**
> **Severity of symptoms depends on the RATE of rise as much as the absolute level** — a slowly rising calcium in primary hyperparathyroidism may be well tolerated at a level that would render a patient with malignancy comatose.

> [!danger] The treatment sequence — fluids first, and why
> **1. INTRAVENOUS SALINE, and generously.** These patients are **profoundly volume depleted** — hypercalcaemia impairs renal concentrating ability, causing polyuria, and the nausea and confusion reduce intake. **Rehydration alone lowers calcium substantially by restoring glomerular filtration and calcium excretion**, and it is the single most important initial step.
> **2. BISPHOSPHONATE** — the mainstay for hypercalcaemia of malignancy, but **it takes two to four days to work**, so it does not replace fluids. Dose-adjust in renal impairment.
> **3. CALCITONIN** — works within hours, useful as a bridge, but **tachyphylaxis develops within a couple of days**, so it buys time rather than providing definitive control.
> **4. DENOSUMAB** — effective, particularly in bisphosphonate-refractory disease and in renal impairment.
> **5. CORTICOSTEROIDS** — specifically for **granulomatous disease, lymphoma and vitamin D toxicity**, where the mechanism is excess calcitriol. **They do not work in hyperparathyroidism or in most solid tumour hypercalcaemia.**
> **6. Dialysis** in extremis or severe renal failure.
> **7. Treat the underlying cause**, and **stop the contributing drugs — thiazides, lithium, calcium and vitamin D supplements.**
> **Loop diuretics are NO LONGER routinely recommended** — the old "saline and frusemide" teaching has been abandoned, because it worsens volume depletion. **Use them only if the patient becomes fluid overloaded during rehydration.**
> `UNVERIFIED — all agents, doses, fluid rates and the severity thresholds triggering each step.`

> [!warning] Hypercalcaemia of malignancy carries a poor prognosis
> Its development in a patient with cancer is a marker of advanced disease and **short median survival.**
> **This should prompt a goals-of-care conversation** alongside treating the calcium — treating the biochemistry repeatedly without addressing the trajectory serves the patient poorly. Cross-refer [[GER2]] 0.5 and [[J5]].

**Ix:** **Corrected calcium (or ionised calcium), phosphate, albumin, PTH, magnesium, UEC, ALP and 25-hydroxyvitamin D — sent together** (*why:* the PTH determines the branch, and the phosphate and ALP refine it; *what:* the pattern). **24-hour urinary calcium or calcium-creatinine clearance ratio** (*why:* **excludes FHH before any consideration of surgery**; *what:* low urinary calcium in FHH). **PTH-related peptide** where malignancy is suspected with a suppressed PTH (*why:* confirms humoral hypercalcaemia of malignancy; *what:* elevated PTHrP). **Serum protein electrophoresis, free light chains and a skeletal survey or whole-body imaging** (*why:* **myeloma presents with hypercalcaemia, renal impairment, anaemia and bone pain — the CRAB features — and is missed if electrophoresis is not specifically requested**; *what:* paraprotein, lytic lesions — cross-refer [[J4]]). ECG (*why:* short QT and arrhythmia; *what:* QT interval). Chest imaging and serum ACE where granulomatous disease is considered (*why:* sarcoidosis and tuberculosis; *what:* hilar lymphadenopathy). TFTs and morning cortisol (*why:* thyrotoxicosis and adrenal insufficiency both cause hypercalcaemia; *what:* abnormal results). Imaging for malignancy directed by the history (*why:* the commonest inpatient cause; *what:* primary tumour, metastases).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
**Intravenous saline.** Stop contributing drugs. Cardiac monitoring in severe hypercalcaemia. Bisphosphonate or denosumab where malignancy-related, calcitonin as a bridge if severe.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the cause — parathyroidectomy, oncological management, corticosteroids for granulomatous disease.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Monitoring, and — where the cause is malignancy — goals-of-care discussion.

---

## 0.3 Hypocalcaemia

> [!tip] Causes
> **· Post-surgical hypoparathyroidism — the commonest cause**, after total thyroidectomy, parathyroidectomy or radical neck surgery. **It may be transient or permanent.**
> **· HYPOMAGNESAEMIA — see the danger box.**
> **· Vitamin D deficiency** — the commonest cause in the community.
> **· Chronic kidney disease** — reduced calcitriol production and phosphate retention.
> **· Autoimmune hypoparathyroidism**, including as part of autoimmune polyglandular syndrome.
> **· Acute pancreatitis** — calcium saponification by released lipase. Cross-refer [[C7]] 0.1.
> **· Rhabdomyolysis and tumour lysis syndrome** — hyperphosphataemia binding calcium.
> **· Massive transfusion** — citrate chelation. Cross-refer [[A9]] 0.2.
> **· "Hungry bone syndrome"** — rapid skeletal uptake of calcium after parathyroidectomy for severe hyperparathyroidism.
> **· Drugs** — bisphosphonates, denosumab, proton pump inhibitors (via magnesium), foscarnet.
> **· Pseudohypoparathyroidism** — PTH resistance, with high PTH and low calcium, and characteristic skeletal features.
> **· Critical illness, sepsis and alkalosis.**

> [!danger] Correct the magnesium, or the calcium will not correct
> **Magnesium depletion causes a functional hypoparathyroidism: it impairs PTH SECRETION and causes PTH RESISTANCE at the target tissues.**
> **So hypocalcaemia due to hypomagnesaemia will NOT respond to calcium replacement until the magnesium is corrected.** The same mechanism makes hypokalaemia refractory in magnesium depletion.
> **Any refractory hypocalcaemia or hypokalaemia should prompt a magnesium level** — this is one of the highest-yield practical points in electrolyte management, and it is repeatedly missed while calcium and potassium are given without effect. See 0.6.

> [!warning] Symptoms and signs
> **Neuromuscular irritability is the theme: perioral and acral paraesthesia · muscle cramps · carpopedal spasm and TETANY · laryngospasm and bronchospasm · seizures.**
> **· Chvostek sign** — tapping the facial nerve produces facial twitching. **Present in a proportion of normal people, so it is sensitive but not specific.**
> **· Trousseau sign** — inflating a blood pressure cuff above systolic pressure for a few minutes induces carpal spasm. **More specific than Chvostek**, and the better bedside test.
> **· Cardiac — PROLONGED QT, with the risk of torsades de pointes, and impaired myocardial contractility.**
> **· Chronic hypocalcaemia — cataracts, basal ganglia calcification, dry skin, brittle nails, and papilloedema.**

> [!danger] Post-thyroidectomy hypocalcaemia — anticipate it
> **Any patient after total thyroidectomy or parathyroid surgery needs calcium monitoring**, because the parathyroids may be bruised, devascularised or removed.
> **Symptoms typically appear within the first day or two**, and the patient should be asked specifically about **perioral tingling and hand cramps** rather than waiting for tetany.
> **Warn the patient before discharge what to look for and when to return** — this is a specific and examinable discharge instruction.

> [!tip] Treatment — and the reason calcitriol is used in hypoparathyroidism
> **Severe or symptomatic hypocalcaemia: intravenous calcium with cardiac monitoring.** **Calcium gluconate is preferred peripherally; calcium chloride is more concentrated but far more irritant and causes tissue necrosis on extravasation, so it is given centrally.**
> **Correct magnesium simultaneously.**
> **Maintenance: oral calcium plus vitamin D — and in HYPOPARATHYROIDISM specifically, ACTIVE vitamin D (calcitriol or alfacalcidol) is required, not cholecalciferol.**
> **The reason: the conversion of 25-hydroxyvitamin D to active calcitriol requires renal 1-alpha-hydroxylase, and that enzyme is PTH-DEPENDENT.** Without PTH, giving plain vitamin D does not produce active hormone. **The same applies in chronic kidney disease**, where the enzyme is lost with renal mass.
> `UNVERIFIED — all doses, infusion rates and monitoring.`

**Ix:** **Corrected or ionised calcium, MAGNESIUM, phosphate, PTH, UEC, ALP and 25-hydroxyvitamin D** (*why:* magnesium in particular, because without it the calcium will not correct; *what:* the pattern). **ECG** (*why:* prolonged QT with risk of torsades; *what:* QT interval). Lipase where pancreatitis is possible (*why:* a cause of hypocalcaemia and a severity marker within it; *what:* elevation). CK and phosphate where rhabdomyolysis or tumour lysis is suspected (*why:* hyperphosphataemia binding calcium; *what:* raised CK and phosphate). Coeliac serology and malabsorption workup where vitamin D deficiency is unexplained (*why:* malabsorption is a treatable underlying cause; *what:* coeliac serology).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
**Intravenous calcium with cardiac monitoring for tetany, seizures, laryngospasm or a markedly prolonged QT. Correct magnesium.**

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Oral calcium with **active** vitamin D in hypoparathyroidism, cholecalciferol in simple deficiency, and treatment of the underlying cause.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Monitoring of calcium, phosphate, renal function and **urinary calcium** in treated hypoparathyroidism — **because treatment increases urinary calcium excretion and risks nephrocalcinosis and stones**, so the target calcium is deliberately kept at the lower end of normal rather than fully normalised.

---

## 0.4 Parathyroid Disorders

> [!tip] Primary hyperparathyroidism
> **Autonomous PTH secretion — a single adenoma in the large majority, four-gland hyperplasia in a minority, and parathyroid carcinoma rarely.**
> **Associated with MEN1 and MEN2A**, so **a young patient, multi-gland disease, or a family history should prompt genetic consideration.** Cross-refer [[I4]].
> **Most cases are now detected incidentally on biochemistry in an asymptomatic patient**, rather than presenting with symptomatic disease.
> **Complications that determine whether to operate: osteoporosis (characteristically affecting CORTICAL bone, so the DISTAL RADIUS is the site to measure — a site frequently omitted from a standard DXA), nephrolithiasis and nephrocalcinosis, declining renal function, and neurocognitive symptoms.**

> [!danger] Localisation imaging is for surgical planning, not for diagnosis
> **The diagnosis of primary hyperparathyroidism is BIOCHEMICAL — hypercalcaemia with an inappropriately normal or raised PTH, after excluding FHH.**
> **Sestamibi scanning, ultrasound and 4D-CT are performed to guide a minimally invasive operation. A negative scan does NOT exclude the diagnosis**, and it does not mean the patient should not be operated on — it means the surgeon may need a bilateral exploration.
> **Ordering imaging to "confirm" hyperparathyroidism is a category error**, and a normal scan in a patient with clear biochemistry is a common source of inappropriate reassurance.

> [!tip] Who gets surgery
> **Parathyroidectomy is curative.** Indications are based on **age, the degree of hypercalcaemia, renal function and urinary calcium, the presence of stones, bone density, and symptoms** — with younger patients and those with complications favoured for surgery, and older asymptomatic patients with mild disease often monitored. `UNVERIFIED — the specific criteria.`
> **Cinacalcet** (a calcimimetic) lowers calcium where surgery is not appropriate, but does not improve bone density.
> **Post-operatively, anticipate hypocalcaemia** — both transient and from hungry bone syndrome. See 0.3.

> [!info] Secondary and tertiary hyperparathyroidism
> **· Secondary** — an APPROPRIATE PTH response to a low or normal calcium. **Chronic kidney disease (reduced calcitriol, phosphate retention) and vitamin D deficiency.** **PTH high, calcium low or normal.** Treat the cause.
> **· Tertiary** — after prolonged secondary stimulation the glands become AUTONOMOUS. **PTH high AND calcium high**, typically in long-standing chronic kidney disease or after transplantation.
> **The distinction is made by the calcium: appropriate response (low/normal calcium) versus autonomy (high calcium).**

> [!warning] Hypoparathyroidism
> Most commonly **post-surgical.** Also autoimmune, congenital (**DiGeorge syndrome**), infiltrative, and functional from magnesium depletion.
> **Low calcium, HIGH phosphate (because PTH normally promotes phosphate excretion), and low or inappropriately normal PTH.**
> **Pseudohypoparathyroidism** — end-organ resistance, so **PTH is HIGH with a low calcium**, and there may be characteristic skeletal features including short fourth metacarpals.

**Ix:** As per 0.2 and 0.3. **24-hour urinary calcium before surgical referral** (*why:* excludes FHH; *what:* low urinary calcium). **DXA including the DISTAL RADIUS** (*why:* primary hyperparathyroidism preferentially affects cortical bone and the radius may be the only abnormal site; *what:* cortical bone loss). Renal imaging (*why:* nephrolithiasis and nephrocalcinosis are surgical indications and are often asymptomatic; *what:* stones, calcification). **Sestamibi, ultrasound or 4D-CT — only once the diagnosis is biochemically established and surgery is planned** (*why:* localisation for a targeted operation; *what:* adenoma location). Genetic assessment where young, multi-gland, or a family history (*why:* MEN syndromes have implications for the patient and family; *what:* pathogenic variant).

---

## 0.5 Metabolic Bone Disease

> [!tip] Osteomalacia and rickets — defective MINERALISATION
> **Osteoporosis is too little normal bone. Osteomalacia is normal quantity, poorly mineralised bone.** The distinction determines the treatment entirely.
> **Cause: vitamin D deficiency predominantly**, and also phosphate depletion and rare disorders of alkaline phosphatase.
> **Presentation: bone pain (often diffuse and mistaken for fibromyalgia), PROXIMAL MYOPATHY with a waddling gait and difficulty rising from a chair or climbing stairs, and fractures.** **Looser zones (pseudofractures)** on imaging.
> **The biochemistry: low or low-normal calcium, LOW PHOSPHATE, HIGH ALKALINE PHOSPHATASE, high PTH, and low 25-hydroxyvitamin D.** **The raised ALP with proximal weakness and bone pain is the pattern to recognise** — it is a treatable cause of weakness that gets attributed to age, deconditioning or fibromyalgia. Cross-refer [[B6]] 0.6 and [[L3]].
> **Rickets** is the childhood equivalent, affecting the growth plates: **bowing of the legs, widened wrists and ankles, rachitic rosary, craniotabes, delayed fontanelle closure and growth failure.** Cross-refer [[M4]].

> [!warning] Vitamin D deficiency in Australia — who is at risk
> Despite the climate: **people with darker skin · those who veil or otherwise cover for cultural or religious reasons · housebound, institutionalised and residential aged care residents · those who avoid sun for medical reasons or because of skin cancer risk · malabsorption (coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, bariatric surgery) · obesity (sequestration in adipose tissue) · anticonvulsants and other enzyme inducers · chronic kidney and liver disease · and exclusively breastfed infants of deficient mothers.**
> **There is a genuine tension between sun exposure for vitamin D and skin cancer prevention in Australia**, given the highest melanoma rates in the world — which is why **supplementation rather than deliberate sun exposure is the usual advice for at-risk groups.** `UNVERIFIED — current supplementation recommendations and target levels.`

> [!tip] Paget disease of bone
> **Disordered, accelerated bone remodelling producing structurally abnormal bone.**
> **Most cases are ASYMPTOMATIC and detected incidentally as an ISOLATED RAISED ALKALINE PHOSPHATASE with a NORMAL calcium and phosphate** — that combination in an older person should prompt the thought.
> When symptomatic: **bone pain, deformity (bowing of long bones, increasing skull size — "my hat doesn't fit"), pathological fracture, secondary osteoarthritis, and DEAFNESS from compression of the eighth nerve as the skull base remodels.** Rarely, high-output cardiac failure, and **osteosarcoma, which is rare but is the reason for investigating new severe pain in a Pagetic bone.**
> **Diagnosis: raised ALP, characteristic radiographic appearances, and a bone scan to define the extent.** **Confirm the ALP is of bone origin — a raised ALP with a normal GGT points away from the liver.** Cross-refer [[C3]] 0.1.
> Treated with **bisphosphonates** where symptomatic or at sites of complication risk.

> [!tip] Chronic kidney disease–mineral and bone disorder
> A combined disturbance of **calcium, phosphate, PTH and vitamin D**, with **bone abnormality and vascular calcification.**
> **Mechanism: falling GFR → phosphate retention and reduced calcitriol production → hypocalcaemia → secondary hyperparathyroidism**, with FGF23 elevation early in the sequence.
> **The consequences that matter clinically are vascular — the calcium-phosphate product drives arterial and valvular calcification, and cardiovascular death far exceeds bone complications as the outcome.**
> **Calciphylaxis** — painful necrotic skin lesions from cutaneous arteriolar calcification, in dialysis patients, with high mortality. **It is misdiagnosed as cellulitis or vasculitis.** Cross-refer [[K2]] 0.1 and [[H3]].
> Management involves **phosphate binders, dietary phosphate restriction, active vitamin D analogues and calcimimetics**, under nephrology direction.

**Ix:** **Calcium, phosphate, ALP, PTH and 25-hydroxyvitamin D together** (*why:* the pattern distinguishes osteomalacia, hyperparathyroidism, Paget disease and renal bone disease; *what:* the combination). **GGT alongside ALP** (*why:* confirms a raised ALP is of bone rather than hepatic origin; *what:* normal GGT with raised ALP indicates bone). Radiographs (*why:* Looser zones in osteomalacia, and the characteristic lytic and sclerotic changes of Paget disease; *what:* the appearances). **Bone scan in Paget disease** (*why:* defines the extent and identifies asymptomatic sites at risk; *what:* focal increased uptake). DXA (*why:* coexisting osteoporosis; *what:* bone density — cross-refer [[GER1]] 0.6). Coeliac serology and malabsorption assessment (*why:* a treatable cause of vitamin D deficiency; *what:* positive serology). Renal function (*why:* CKD-MBD; *what:* eGFR).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Not usually required.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
**Vitamin D and calcium replacement for osteomalacia — with a dramatic response in the proximal myopathy**, which is one of the more satisfying treatments in medicine. Bisphosphonates for symptomatic Paget disease. Phosphate binders and specialist management in CKD-MBD.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Maintenance vitamin D, treatment of the underlying malabsorption, hearing assessment in skull-base Paget disease, and falls and fracture prevention. Cross-refer [[GER1]] 0.5–0.6.

---

## 0.6 Phosphate and Magnesium

> [!danger] Hypophosphataemia — the causes that matter in hospital
> **· REFEEDING SYNDROME — the classic and most important. Reintroducing nutrition after prolonged starvation drives insulin release and a rapid intracellular shift of phosphate, potassium and magnesium.** Cross-refer [[C2]] 0.7 and [[GER2]] 0.3.
> **· Treatment of diabetic ketoacidosis** — insulin drives phosphate intracellularly.
> **· Alcohol use disorder** — through poor intake, magnesium depletion and renal losses.
> **· Respiratory alkalosis.**
> **· Hyperparathyroidism, Fanconi syndrome, and tumour-induced osteomalacia (FGF23-mediated).**
> **· Certain intravenous IRON preparations cause FGF23-mediated renal phosphate wasting and clinically significant hypophosphataemia** — an increasingly recognised and under-appreciated cause, relevant given how widely IV iron is now used.
> **The consequences are under-recognised: muscle weakness including DIAPHRAGMATIC weakness and FAILURE TO WEAN from ventilation · rhabdomyolysis · haemolysis · impaired myocardial contractility · confusion, seizures and coma.**
> **A patient who will not wean from a ventilator deserves a phosphate level.**

> [!tip] Hyperphosphataemia
> **· Chronic kidney disease — by far the commonest.**
> **· Tumour lysis syndrome and rhabdomyolysis** — massive cellular release.
> **· Hypoparathyroidism.**
> **· Phosphate-containing enemas and laxatives** — which can cause severe, occasionally fatal, hyperphosphataemia and hypocalcaemia, **particularly in older people and those with renal impairment**, and are a genuine iatrogenic hazard.
> Consequences: hypocalcaemia by precipitation, and **vascular and soft tissue calcification** with chronicity.

> [!danger] Hypomagnesaemia — the electrolyte that makes the others refractory
> **· Proton pump inhibitors — an under-recognised and increasingly common cause with long-term use, and it resolves on stopping the drug.**
> **· Diuretics (loop and thiazide) · alcohol · diarrhoea and malabsorption · refeeding · poorly controlled diabetes · and drugs — aminoglycosides, amphotericin, cisplatin, calcineurin inhibitors, and cetuximab.**
> **The critical clinical point: magnesium depletion causes REFRACTORY HYPOKALAEMIA and REFRACTORY HYPOCALCAEMIA.**
> **Low magnesium promotes renal potassium wasting and impairs both PTH secretion and PTH action — so potassium and calcium will not correct until the magnesium is replaced, no matter how much you give.**
> **Any potassium or calcium that will not correct despite adequate replacement should prompt a magnesium level.** This is among the most practically useful facts in electrolyte management and it is repeatedly overlooked while replacement is escalated fruitlessly.
> **Consequences: arrhythmia including TORSADES DE POINTES (cross-refer [[B3]] 0.3), tetany, tremor, weakness, seizures and personality change.**

> [!warning] Hypermagnesaemia
> Almost always **renal impairment combined with a magnesium load** — magnesium-containing antacids and laxatives, or **therapeutic magnesium in obstetrics for pre-eclampsia and eclampsia.**
> **In obstetric magnesium therapy, LOSS OF DEEP TENDON REFLEXES is the early warning sign of toxicity and precedes respiratory depression and cardiac arrest — which is why reflexes are monitored during infusion.** **Calcium gluconate is the antidote.** Cross-refer [[O2]].
> Progression: loss of reflexes → hypotension and bradycardia → respiratory depression → cardiac arrest.

**Ix:** **Phosphate and magnesium measured alongside calcium and potassium** (*why:* they interact, and measuring one in isolation leads to fruitless replacement of another; *what:* the full electrolyte picture). **Magnesium in any refractory hypokalaemia or hypocalcaemia** (*why:* the replacement will not work until it is corrected; *what:* low magnesium). Renal function (*why:* determines both clearance and the safety of replacement; *what:* eGFR). **Urinary phosphate and magnesium** where the cause is unclear (*why:* **distinguishes renal wasting from gastrointestinal loss or redistribution**, which have different causes and management; *what:* fractional excretion). Medication review (*why:* PPIs, diuretics, IV iron and chemotherapy agents are the commonest reversible causes; *what:* culprit drug). ECG (*why:* torsades risk with hypomagnesaemia, and conduction effects with hypermagnesaemia; *what:* QT, conduction). **Deep tendon reflexes during therapeutic magnesium infusion** (*why:* the earliest clinical sign of toxicity; *what:* reflex loss).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Intravenous replacement for severe or symptomatic deficiency with cardiac monitoring. **Calcium gluconate for magnesium toxicity.** Stop the causative agent.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the cause. **Anticipate and prevent refeeding syndrome in at-risk patients rather than treating it after it occurs.**

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Review long-term PPI and diuretic use. Monitor in ongoing losses and in chronic kidney disease.

---

> Osteoporosis and fracture prevention → [[GER1]] 0.6 · Falls → [[GER1]] 0.5 · Refeeding syndrome → [[C2]] 0.7 and [[GER2]] 0.3 · Alkalosis and ionised calcium → [[F0.2]] 0.7 · Massive transfusion and citrate → [[A9]] 0.2 · Pancreatitis → [[C7]] 0.1 · Rhabdomyolysis and tumour lysis → [[A7]] 0.6 and [[J5]] · Torsades and QT prolongation → [[B3]] 0.3 · Myeloma → [[J4]] · Chronic kidney disease and CKD-MBD → [[H3]] · Calciphylaxis and pseudocellulitis → [[K2]] 0.1 · Raised ALP interpretation → [[C3]] 0.1 · Proximal myopathy → [[B6]] 0.6 and [[L3]] · MEN syndromes and phaeochromocytoma → [[I4]] and [[B2]] 0.4 · Obstetric magnesium therapy → [[O2]] · Rickets and growth → [[M4]]
