> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Physiology, diagnostic logic and clinical reasoning are reliable at intern level. **Every dose, threshold, cut-off, imaging criterion and test protocol carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify against **eTG Endocrinology**, the **Australian Medicines Handbook**, and endocrine society guidance — **dynamic test protocols (synacthen, dexamethasone suppression, OGTT for GH) are laboratory-specific and must come from your own laboratory.**
>
> **Adrenal crisis is built in [[F0.3]] 0.6**, and **primary aldosteronism and phaeochromocytoma are introduced in [[B2]] 0.4** — both are cross-referenced and extended here rather than repeated.

---

## 0.1 The Pituitary and Hypopituitarism

**A/P:** The **anterior pituitary** secretes **ACTH, TSH, LH and FSH, growth hormone and prolactin** under hypothalamic control via the portal system. The **posterior pituitary** stores and releases **ADH and oxytocin**, which are synthesised in the hypothalamus.
**Prolactin is unique in being under TONIC INHIBITION by dopamine** — every other anterior pituitary hormone is under stimulatory control. **This single fact explains two clinical phenomena**: dopamine antagonist drugs raise prolactin, and **a lesion compressing the pituitary stalk causes prolactin to RISE while every other hormone falls.**

> [!tip] The order of hormone loss in a compressive lesion
> Classically **growth hormone → gonadotrophins (LH, FSH) → TSH → ACTH**, remembered as **"Go Look For The Adenoma."**
> **So the earliest features are growth hormone deficiency (non-specific fatigue, reduced quality of life) and hypogonadism (loss of libido, amenorrhoea, erectile dysfunction, loss of body hair)** — which is why pituitary disease is typically present for years before diagnosis, the symptoms having been attributed to ageing, stress or depression.
> **A raised prolactin with low other pituitary hormones suggests stalk compression rather than a prolactinoma** — and the distinction matters because the treatments differ. See 0.2.

> [!warning] Causes of hypopituitarism
> **· Pituitary adenoma and its treatment — surgery and radiotherapy.**
> **· Pituitary apoplexy** — see below.
> **· Sheehan syndrome** — pituitary infarction following major postpartum haemorrhage. **Classically presents with FAILURE TO LACTATE and failure to resume menses**, though it may present years later with insidious hypopituitarism. **Ask about the obstetric history in any woman with unexplained hypopituitarism.**
> **· Traumatic brain injury** — a genuinely under-recognised cause. Pituitary dysfunction after significant head injury is common and is frequently missed because the symptoms are attributed to the brain injury itself.
> **· Infiltrative and inflammatory** — sarcoidosis, tuberculosis, haemochromatosis, Langerhans cell histiocytosis, **lymphocytic hypophysitis** (including in pregnancy) and **immune checkpoint inhibitor hypophysitis**, which is increasingly common and often permanent. Cross-refer [[A5]] 0.7.
> **· Craniopharyngioma, metastases, empty sella, and genetic causes.**

> [!danger] Secondary adrenal insufficiency looks different from primary — and the differences are diagnostic
> **· NO hyperpigmentation** — because there is no ACTH excess (in primary disease, the POMC precursor of ACTH stimulates melanocytes).
> **· NORMAL potassium** — because **aldosterone is regulated by the renin-angiotensin system, not by ACTH**, so mineralocorticoid function is preserved. **Hyperkalaemia therefore points to PRIMARY adrenal failure.**
> **· Hyponatraemia still occurs**, through cortisol deficiency causing inappropriate ADH release.
> **· Hypotension is usually less profound** than in primary disease.

> [!danger] Give hydrocortisone before thyroxine
> **In any patient with combined pituitary hormone deficiency, replace glucocorticoid FIRST.**
> **Thyroid hormone increases cortisol clearance and metabolic demand, so giving thyroxine to a patient with unrecognised adrenal insufficiency can precipitate an adrenal crisis.** The same principle appears in myxoedema coma. Cross-refer [[I1]] 0.1 and 0.4.

> [!danger] Pituitary apoplexy
> **Sudden haemorrhage or infarction into a pituitary tumour** — often the first presentation of a previously unknown adenoma.
> **Presentation: abrupt severe headache (frequently thunderclap), visual field loss, ophthalmoplegia from cavernous sinus involvement (III, IV, VI), reduced consciousness, and meningism** — and it is **misdiagnosed as subarachnoid haemorrhage or meningitis.** Cross-refer [[D1]] 0.2 and [[D7]] 0.6.
> **The immediate priority is HYDROCORTISONE** — acute ACTH deficiency causes adrenal crisis and is the reversible cause of death. **Give it before imaging confirms anything.**
> Then urgent MRI, ophthalmological assessment of fields and acuity, and neurosurgical involvement, with surgery for visual compromise or deteriorating consciousness.

> [!tip] Posterior pituitary — diabetes insipidus
> **Cranial (central) DI** — deficient ADH: pituitary surgery, tumour, trauma, infiltration.
> **Nephrogenic DI** — renal resistance to ADH: **lithium (the commonest), hypercalcaemia, hypokalaemia**, chronic kidney disease.
> **Polyuria and polydipsia with dilute urine despite a rising serum osmolality.** Distinguished from primary polydipsia by water deprivation testing or copeptin measurement, and from cranial DI by the response to desmopressin.
> **After pituitary surgery, a triphasic response can occur — transient DI, then a phase of SIADH as stored hormone is released, then permanent DI** — so **a post-operative patient can swing from hypernatraemia to hyponatraemia and back**, which is confusing if not anticipated. Cross-refer [[H3]].

**S/Smx:** Insidious and non-specific — **fatigue, weight change, loss of libido, amenorrhoea, erectile dysfunction, loss of axillary and pubic hair, pallor with fine wrinkled skin, cold intolerance, postural dizziness, and hypoglycaemia.** Plus mass effect: **headache and bitemporal visual field loss.**

**Ix:** **Paired anterior pituitary hormones with their target hormones, taken in the morning — cortisol with ACTH, free T4 with TSH, LH and FSH with testosterone or oestradiol, prolactin, and IGF-1** (*why:* **a target hormone must be interpreted alongside its trophic hormone — a low free T4 with a non-elevated TSH is central hypothyroidism, whereas the free T4 alone looks like ordinary hypothyroidism**; *what:* the paired pattern). **Short synacthen test** (*why:* assesses the adrenal axis, though it may be falsely normal in very recent-onset secondary insufficiency before adrenal atrophy develops; *what:* cortisol response). **MRI pituitary with dedicated sequences** (*why:* CT is inadequate for the sella; *what:* adenoma, stalk, apoplexy, infiltration). **Formal visual field assessment** (*why:* **bitemporal hemianopia may be asymptomatic until advanced, and its presence or progression drives the surgical decision**; *what:* field defect). Sodium and osmolality, paired with urine osmolality (*why:* DI and SIADH; *what:* the pattern). Ferritin and transferrin saturation, ACE and calcium (*why:* haemochromatosis and sarcoidosis as infiltrative causes; *what:* the underlying disease).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
**Hydrocortisone for suspected apoplexy or crisis, before confirmation.**

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Replace the deficient hormones — **glucocorticoid first, then thyroxine, then sex steroids and growth hormone as indicated.** Surgery for mass effect.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Sick day rules, an emergency hydrocortisone supply, a steroid card and a medical alert** — see 0.3. Lifelong endocrine follow-up, fertility discussion, and bone protection.

---

## 0.2 Pituitary Tumours

> [!tip] Mass effect
> **· Bitemporal hemianopia** from compression of the decussating nasal fibres at the optic chiasm — **the visual field defect that is nearly pathognomonic of a suprasellar mass.**
> **· Cranial nerve palsies (III, IV, V1, V2, VI) with lateral extension into the cavernous sinus** — cross-refer [[D7]] 0.6.
> **· Headache · hypopituitarism · and hydrocephalus with very large lesions.**

> [!danger] Prolactinoma — check the simple things before ordering an MRI
> **Hyperprolactinaemia has many causes and most are not a tumour:**
> **· PREGNANCY — do a pregnancy test.**
> **· DRUGS — antipsychotics (particularly risperidone and amisulpride), metoclopramide and prochlorperazine, SSRIs, opioids, verapamil, and oestrogens.** **The drug chart explains a large proportion of raised prolactins.**
> **· PRIMARY HYPOTHYROIDISM** — TRH stimulates prolactin release. **Check TFTs**; the prolactin normalises with thyroxine.
> **· Stress, venepuncture itself, chest wall lesions, nipple stimulation, renal failure, cirrhosis.**
> **· Stalk compression by ANY sellar mass** — producing a modestly raised prolactin with other hormone deficiencies.
> **Two assay pitfalls worth knowing:**
> **· Macroprolactin** — a biologically inactive complexed form causing a spuriously raised result in an asymptomatic patient. Ask the laboratory to screen for it.
> **· The "hook effect"** — **in a very large macroprolactinoma the prolactin can be SO high that it saturates the assay and produces a FALSELY LOW or normal result.** **So a large pituitary mass with an unimpressive prolactin should prompt a request for serial dilution** — otherwise a prolactinoma is misclassified as a non-functioning adenoma and taken to surgery unnecessarily.

> [!warning] Prolactinoma is the pituitary tumour treated MEDICALLY first
> **Dopamine agonists (cabergoline, bromocriptine) are first-line even for large macroadenomas**, and they shrink the tumour as well as normalising prolactin — often dramatically, with recovery of visual fields.
> **This is the exception: every other pituitary tumour is treated surgically first.** Getting it the wrong way round means an unnecessary operation.
> Presentation differs by sex: **women present earlier with amenorrhoea, galactorrhoea and infertility from a microadenoma; men present later with a macroadenoma and mass effect**, because hypogonadal symptoms are attributed to other things.

> [!tip] Acromegaly — look at old photographs
> **Growth hormone excess after epiphyseal fusion.** Onset is so insidious that neither the patient nor their family notices — **comparing current appearance with photographs from ten and twenty years earlier is genuinely the most useful diagnostic manoeuvre**, and a driver's licence or wedding photo often makes the diagnosis in the room.
> **Features: coarsening facial features, prognathism with dental separation, macroglossia, enlarging hands and feet (increasing ring and shoe size — ask), excessive sweating, arthralgia and arthropathy, CARPAL TUNNEL SYNDROME, obstructive sleep apnoea, hypertension, diabetes, cardiomyopathy, and an increased risk of COLONIC POLYPS AND CARCINOMA.**
> **Cardiovascular disease is the main cause of excess mortality**, and it is largely reversible with treatment.
> **Diagnosis: a RANDOM growth hormone is useless because secretion is pulsatile.** **IGF-1 is the screening test** (an integrated measure), and **failure of GH to suppress during an oral glucose tolerance test is confirmatory.** `UNVERIFIED — thresholds and protocols.`
> Treatment: **transsphenoidal surgery first-line**, with somatostatin analogues, dopamine agonists, and the GH receptor antagonist pegvisomant as adjuncts.

> [!tip] The other tumours
> **· Cushing disease** — an ACTH-secreting pituitary adenoma, usually a microadenoma. See 0.4.
> **· Non-functioning adenoma** — the commonest macroadenoma, presenting with mass effect and hypopituitarism.
> **· Craniopharyngioma** — suprasellar, often calcified, presenting in children and again in older adults, with **a high rate of diabetes insipidus and hypothalamic dysfunction** including obesity and disordered thirst.
> **· TSH-secreting adenoma** — rare, producing the unusual pattern of **raised free T4 with a non-suppressed TSH.** Cross-refer [[I1]] 0.1.

**Ix:** **Prolactin, with pregnancy test, TFTs and medication review FIRST** (*why:* these explain most raised prolactins at no cost; *what:* the reversible cause). Request for macroprolactin screening or serial dilution where the result does not fit the picture (*why:* the two assay pitfalls above; *what:* true prolactin level). **IGF-1** (*why:* the screening test for acromegaly; *what:* elevation for age). **Oral glucose tolerance test with GH measurement** (*why:* confirms acromegaly; *what:* failure of GH suppression). **Full anterior pituitary profile** (*why:* co-existing hypopituitarism determines replacement needs; *what:* deficiencies). **MRI pituitary** (*why:* the imaging test; *what:* adenoma size and extension). **Formal visual field testing** (*why:* determines urgency of surgery; *what:* bitemporal defect). Colonoscopy in acromegaly (*why:* increased colonic neoplasia risk; *what:* polyps), and echocardiography and sleep study (*why:* cardiomyopathy and sleep apnoea are major contributors to morbidity; *what:* structural disease, apnoea-hypopnoea index).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Apoplexy per 0.1.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
**Dopamine agonist for prolactinoma; transsphenoidal surgery for everything else**, with medical and radiotherapy adjuncts.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Surveillance imaging and hormone assessment, replacement of deficiencies, and — after surgery — **monitoring for the triphasic sodium response** described in 0.1.

---

## 0.3 Adrenal Insufficiency

> [!danger] The commonest cause of adrenal insufficiency overall is exogenous corticosteroid
> **Not Addison disease. Not tuberculosis. Prescribed steroids.**
> Any patient on supraphysiological glucocorticoid for more than a few weeks — oral, but also **high-dose inhaled, topical, intra-articular and intranasal** — has a suppressed axis and **cannot mount a stress response.**
> **The consequences: abrupt cessation, or an intercurrent illness or operation without stress dosing, precipitates an adrenal crisis.**
> **Ask about steroids in every patient with unexplained hypotension, hyponatraemia, fatigue or collapse** — including the intra-articular injection they had three weeks ago and do not consider a medication.

> [!tip] Primary versus secondary — the discriminating features
> | | **Primary (Addison)** | **Secondary (pituitary / steroid withdrawal)** |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Pigmentation** | **PRESENT** (buccal mucosa, palmar creases, scars, pressure areas) | **Absent** |
> | **Potassium** | **HIGH** (aldosterone deficient) | **Normal** |
> | **Sodium** | Low | Low |
> | **Salt craving** | Present | Absent |
> | **Aldosterone** | Deficient — **needs fludrocortisone** | Preserved — **hydrocortisone alone** |
> | **ACTH** | **High** | Low or inappropriately normal |
> **Hyperkalaemia and pigmentation together are essentially diagnostic of primary adrenal failure.**

> [!warning] Causes of primary adrenal insufficiency
> **· Autoimmune adrenalitis — the commonest in Australia**, with 21-hydroxylase antibodies, often as part of an autoimmune polyglandular syndrome (with type 1 diabetes, autoimmune thyroid disease, coeliac disease, vitiligo, pernicious anaemia).
> **· Tuberculosis — the commonest cause worldwide**, and still relevant in migrant populations.
> **· Adrenal haemorrhage** — meningococcal sepsis (Waterhouse-Friderichsen), anticoagulation, antiphospholipid syndrome, trauma. Cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.9.
> **· Metastases** (lung, breast, melanoma) and lymphoma.
> **· Congenital adrenal hyperplasia** — see 0.6.
> **· Drugs** — ketoconazole, etomidate (a single induction dose suppresses steroidogenesis), and **immune checkpoint inhibitors.** Cross-refer [[A5]] 0.7.
> **· Adrenoleukodystrophy** in young males.

**S/Smx:** **Fatigue, weight loss, anorexia, nausea and vomiting, abdominal pain (which can mimic an acute abdomen), postural dizziness, salt craving, myalgia and arthralgia, and hyperpigmentation in primary disease.** The onset is insidious and the diagnosis is frequently made only when a crisis occurs.

> [!danger] Sick day rules and the emergency kit — the OSCE-critical education
> **Every patient on long-term glucocorticoid replacement must know:**
> **1. "Double the dose" during febrile illness**, and continue until recovery. `UNVERIFIED — the exact sick day dosing rules.`
> **2. "If you cannot keep tablets down — vomiting or diarrhoea — you need an INJECTION."** They must have **injectable hydrocortisone at home, and they and a family member must be trained to give it.**
> **3. A steroid emergency card and a medical alert bracelet.**
> **4. Extra cover for surgery, dental procedures and major stress**, arranged in advance.
> **5. Seek urgent help for vomiting, diarrhoea, or feeling profoundly unwell.**
> **This education is what prevents death from adrenal crisis, and it is frequently given once at diagnosis and never revisited.** Check they still have in-date injectable hydrocortisone and can describe when to use it.

**Ix:** **Morning (8–9 am) cortisol** (*why:* the screening test, exploiting the diurnal peak — a clearly high value excludes and a clearly low value confirms, with intermediate values requiring dynamic testing; *what:* low, indeterminate or reassuring). **Short synacthen test** (*why:* the confirmatory test; *what:* failure of cortisol to rise adequately — noting it **may be falsely normal in very recent secondary insufficiency** before the adrenals atrophy). **ACTH, taken with the cortisol** (*why:* **distinguishes primary from secondary, which determines whether fludrocortisone is needed and whether the pituitary needs imaging**; *what:* high in primary, low or normal in secondary). **UEC** (*why:* the sodium and potassium pattern is itself diagnostic; *what:* hyponatraemia, hyperkalaemia in primary). Glucose (*why:* hypoglycaemia; *what:* low glucose). **21-hydroxylase antibodies** (*why:* confirms autoimmune aetiology and prompts screening for associated autoimmune disease; *what:* positive). **Adrenal CT where antibodies are negative** (*why:* identifies haemorrhage, infiltration, metastases or tuberculosis; *what:* adrenal enlargement, calcification, mass). **MRI pituitary where secondary and not explained by steroid use** (*why:* pituitary pathology; *what:* lesion). Full pituitary profile in secondary disease (*why:* other axes are usually also affected; *what:* additional deficiencies).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
**Adrenal crisis: hydrocortisone and fluid resuscitation immediately, before confirmation. Take the cortisol first only if it costs no delay.** Cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.6.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
**Hydrocortisone in divided doses mimicking the diurnal rhythm, plus FLUDROCORTISONE in PRIMARY disease only.** `UNVERIFIED — doses and regimens.`

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Sick day rules, emergency injectable hydrocortisone, steroid card, medical alert — revisited at every review.** Screening for associated autoimmune disease. Bone protection. Fertility and pregnancy planning (requirements increase in pregnancy).

---

## 0.4 Cushing Syndrome

> [!danger] Again — the commonest cause is prescribed glucocorticoid
> **Exogenous steroid vastly outnumbers all endogenous causes combined.** Establish it before embarking on any investigation, **including inhaled, topical, intranasal, intra-articular and complementary preparations** (some "herbal" arthritis remedies contain undeclared steroids).

> [!tip] Endogenous causes, split by ACTH
> **ACTH-DEPENDENT (ACTH raised or inappropriately normal):**
> **· Cushing DISEASE — a pituitary ACTH-secreting adenoma. The commonest endogenous cause.**
> **· Ectopic ACTH — small cell lung carcinoma, bronchial and other neuroendocrine tumours.**
> **ACTH-INDEPENDENT (ACTH suppressed):**
> **· Adrenal adenoma, adrenal carcinoma, or bilateral adrenal hyperplasia.**
> **Note the terminology: "Cushing DISEASE" means the pituitary cause specifically; "Cushing SYNDROME" is the clinical state from any cause.**

> [!warning] The discriminating features — separating Cushing from simple obesity
> Central obesity, moon face and a dorsocervical fat pad occur in obesity generally. **The features with genuine discriminating value are those reflecting protein catabolism:**
> **· PROXIMAL MYOPATHY — difficulty rising from a chair without using the arms.**
> **· THIN SKIN and easy BRUISING** — sometimes with skin that tears.
> **· WIDE (over about 1 cm) PURPLE striae**, as opposed to the pale narrow striae of weight gain.
> **· Poor wound healing.**
> Alongside: hypertension, glucose intolerance or diabetes, osteoporosis and fragility fractures, immunosuppression with opportunistic infection, **psychiatric change (depression, psychosis, insomnia, emotional lability)**, and in women hirsutism, acne and oligomenorrhoea.

> [!tip] Ectopic ACTH looks different
> **Rapid onset · marked HYPOKALAEMIC METABOLIC ALKALOSIS (very high cortisol overwhelms the enzyme that protects the mineralocorticoid receptor) · pronounced PIGMENTATION from very high ACTH · muscle weakness · and WEIGHT LOSS rather than gain**, because the underlying malignancy dominates.
> **A patient with hypokalaemic alkalosis, pigmentation, weakness and weight loss does not look cushingoid** — and that is precisely the presentation that gets missed.

> [!warning] The diagnostic sequence, and pseudo-Cushing
> **Step 1 — establish cortisol excess** with at least two abnormal screening tests from: **24-hour urinary free cortisol, late-night salivary cortisol, and the overnight or low-dose dexamethasone suppression test.**
> **PSEUDO-CUSHING states cause false positives: harmful alcohol use, severe depression, obesity, poorly controlled diabetes, and physiological stress.** Testing during an acute illness or in an actively drinking patient produces uninterpretable results.
> **Step 2 — measure ACTH** to determine dependence.
> **Step 3 — localise**: pituitary MRI, adrenal CT, high-dose dexamethasone suppression or CRH testing, and — where the source remains unclear — **inferior petrosal sinus sampling**, which reliably distinguishes pituitary from ectopic ACTH.
> `UNVERIFIED — all test protocols and thresholds.`

**Ix:** **Careful medication history including all routes and complementary products** (*why:* it is the commonest cause and requires no further investigation; *what:* exogenous steroid). **Two concordant screening tests** as above (*why:* single tests have significant false positive and negative rates; *what:* cortisol excess). **ACTH** (*why:* the branch point between pituitary/ectopic and adrenal causes; *what:* raised or suppressed). Potassium and bicarbonate (*why:* hypokalaemic alkalosis suggests ectopic ACTH; *what:* the pattern). **MRI pituitary, CT adrenals, and CT chest and abdomen for an ectopic source** (*why:* localisation determines surgery; *what:* adenoma, adrenal mass, neuroendocrine tumour). Inferior petrosal sinus sampling (*why:* distinguishes pituitary from ectopic where imaging is equivocal, and pituitary microadenomas are frequently not visible on MRI; *what:* central-to-peripheral ACTH gradient). Assessment of complications — glucose, lipids, bone density, blood pressure (*why:* they drive morbidity and need treating in parallel; *what:* diabetes, osteoporosis, hypertension).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Not usually required, except for severe hypokalaemia, uncontrolled hyperglycaemia or psychosis.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
**Surgical — transsphenoidal for Cushing disease, adrenalectomy for adrenal causes, resection of an ectopic source.** Medical therapy (metyrapone, ketoconazole, osilodrostat) to control cortisol before surgery or where surgery is not possible.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**After successful treatment, the contralateral or residual axis is suppressed and glucocorticoid replacement is required — sometimes for a year or more — with sick day rules.** Patients feel dreadful during this period as they withdraw from long-standing hypercortisolism, and **warning them of this in advance prevents them concluding the operation failed.** Monitor for recurrence, and treat the persisting metabolic and bone complications.

---

## 0.5 Mineralocorticoid Excess and Phaeochromocytoma

> [!warning] Primary aldosteronism — commoner than taught, and specifically treatable
> Introduced in [[B2]] 0.4. The points that bear repeating:
> **· It accounts for a substantial minority of hypertension and a larger proportion of resistant hypertension.**
> **· HYPOKALAEMIA IS PRESENT IN ONLY A MINORITY — its absence does NOT exclude the diagnosis.** This is the single most consequential misconception.
> **· Screen with the aldosterone-to-renin ratio**, recognising that **beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics and especially mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists all interfere** — so the drugs the patient is already on are precisely the ones that confound the test.
> **· It carries cardiovascular and renal risk BEYOND that attributable to the blood pressure alone**, through direct aldosterone-mediated fibrosis — which is why identifying it matters even when the blood pressure is controlled.
> **· Confirmatory testing, then adrenal vein sampling to determine whether the source is unilateral (surgically curable by adrenalectomy) or bilateral (treated with a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist).** **CT alone cannot reliably lateralise**, because non-functioning adenomas are common and a visible nodule may not be the source.
> `UNVERIFIED — screening thresholds, drug withdrawal periods, and confirmatory protocols.`

> [!danger] Phaeochromocytoma and paraganglioma
> Introduced in [[B2]] 0.4. Extended here:
> **· The classic triad is EPISODIC headache, palpitations and diaphoresis**, characteristically with **pallor rather than flushing**, on a background of sustained or labile hypertension. Attacks may be provoked by exertion, anaesthesia, palpation of the abdomen, or certain drugs.
> **· ALPHA BLOCKADE MUST PRECEDE BETA BLOCKADE.** Giving a beta-blocker first leaves unopposed alpha-mediated vasoconstriction and can precipitate a hypertensive crisis. This is the single most examined point.
> **· Screen with plasma free metanephrines or 24-hour urinary metanephrines**, which are more sensitive than catecholamines. **False positives occur with tricyclics, levodopa, stimulants, and physiological stress**, so pre-test conditions matter.
> **· GENETIC TESTING IS NOW RECOMMENDED IN ESSENTIALLY ALL CASES** — a substantially higher proportion than historically appreciated carry a germline mutation (**SDHx, VHL, RET, NF1**), with implications for surveillance, for multifocal and malignant disease, and for family screening. **The old "rule of 10s" understates the hereditary proportion considerably.**
> **· Never biopsy an adrenal mass before excluding phaeochromocytoma** — see 0.7.

> [!tip] Other mineralocorticoid excess states
> **· Liquorice** — glycyrrhizic acid inhibits the enzyme that protects the mineralocorticoid receptor from cortisol, producing apparent mineralocorticoid excess with hypertension and hypokalaemia. **Ask about liquorice consumption in unexplained hypokalaemic hypertension** — it is a satisfying and entirely reversible diagnosis.
> **· Liddle syndrome** — an epithelial sodium channel mutation, with hypertension, hypokalaemia and **suppressed aldosterone AND renin.**
> **· Cushing syndrome** with very high cortisol — see 0.4.
> **· Congenital adrenal hyperplasia** variants — see 0.6.

**Ix:** **Aldosterone-to-renin ratio** with attention to interfering drugs (*why:* screens for primary aldosteronism; *what:* raised ratio requiring confirmation). **Plasma free or urinary metanephrines** (*why:* screens for phaeochromocytoma, and must be done before any adrenal intervention; *what:* elevated). **Genetic testing in confirmed phaeochromocytoma or paraganglioma** (*why:* a high proportion are hereditary, with surveillance and family implications; *what:* germline variant). Potassium, bicarbonate and blood pressure (*why:* the hypokalaemic alkalotic hypertensive pattern; *what:* the combination). CT or MRI adrenals (*why:* localisation; *what:* mass). **Adrenal vein sampling in confirmed primary aldosteronism being considered for surgery** (*why:* CT cannot reliably lateralise; *what:* lateralisation).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
**Alpha blockade before beta blockade in a phaeochromocytoma crisis.** Cross-refer [[B2]] 0.3.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Adrenalectomy after adequate alpha blockade for phaeochromocytoma; adrenalectomy or mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist for primary aldosteronism.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Lifelong surveillance and family screening in hereditary phaeochromocytoma.** Continued cardiovascular risk management — the vascular damage accrued before diagnosis does not reverse.

---

## 0.6 Androgen Excess, Hirsutism and Virilisation

> [!tip] Define the problem precisely first
> **· HIRSUTISM — terminal (coarse, pigmented) hair in a MALE pattern in a woman: upper lip, chin, chest, upper abdomen, back, inner thighs. It is ANDROGEN-dependent.** Quantified with the Ferriman-Gallwey score.
> **· HYPERTRICHOSIS — generalised increase in fine vellus hair in a NON-androgenic distribution.** Caused by drugs (**phenytoin, ciclosporin, minoxidil, corticosteroids**), malnutrition, anorexia nervosa, porphyria and paraneoplastic states. **It is a different problem with a different differential and does not warrant an androgen workup.**
> **· VIRILISATION — clitoromegaly, deepening of the voice, male-pattern temporal balding, increased muscle bulk, breast atrophy.** **This is a different order of androgen excess.**

> [!danger] Rapid-onset hirsutism with virilisation is an androgen-secreting tumour until proven otherwise
> **Slowly progressive hirsutism from adolescence is almost always PCOS.**
> **Rapid onset over months, in an older woman, with virilisation, is an ovarian or adrenal androgen-secreting tumour** — and it requires urgent investigation with testosterone, DHEAS and imaging.
> **The tempo and the presence of virilisation are the two features that separate the benign from the sinister**, and asking "how quickly did this happen?" is the key question.

> [!tip] Polycystic ovary syndrome — the commonest cause by a wide margin
> **Diagnosed by the Rotterdam criteria — two of three: oligo- or anovulation · clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism · polycystic ovarian morphology on ultrasound** — **after excluding other causes**, which makes it partly a diagnosis of exclusion. `UNVERIFIED — the criteria and the ultrasound thresholds, and the modified criteria for adolescents, in whom polycystic morphology is common and normal.`
> **The metabolic dimension matters more than the cosmetic one**: insulin resistance, impaired glucose tolerance and type 2 diabetes, metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, obstructive sleep apnoea, dyslipidaemia and cardiovascular risk.
> **And the endometrial risk: chronic anovulation means unopposed oestrogen, which causes endometrial hyperplasia and carcinoma.** **Women with PCOS and prolonged amenorrhoea need endometrial protection** — cyclical progestogen, a combined pill, or a hormonal IUD. **This is frequently overlooked when the focus is on hair and fertility.** Cross-refer [[O4]].

> [!warning] The other causes
> **· Non-classical congenital adrenal hyperplasia (21-hydroxylase deficiency)** — presents in adolescence or adulthood with hirsutism and oligomenorrhoea, indistinguishable from PCOS clinically. **Screened for with an early-morning follicular-phase 17-hydroxyprogesterone**, and it matters because management and genetic counselling differ.
> **· Cushing syndrome** — see 0.4.
> **· Hyperprolactinaemia** and **acromegaly.**
> **· Drugs — anabolic steroids, testosterone (including partner's transdermal gel transferred by skin contact), danazol, and valproate.**
> **· Ovarian hyperthecosis** in postmenopausal women.
> **· Classical congenital adrenal hyperplasia** presenting in the neonate with ambiguous genitalia and salt-wasting crisis — a paediatric emergency. Cross-refer [[M3]].

> [!tip] Male hypogonadism, briefly
> **Primary (testicular) — LOW testosterone with HIGH LH and FSH.** Klinefelter syndrome, orchitis, trauma, chemotherapy, radiation.
> **Secondary (pituitary/hypothalamic) — LOW testosterone with LOW or inappropriately normal LH and FSH.** Pituitary disease, hyperprolactinaemia, **opioids (a major and under-recognised cause), anabolic steroid use, obesity, and systemic illness.**
> **Diagnosis requires a MORNING total testosterone on at least two occasions**, with SHBG where the total is borderline. **Do not diagnose it from a single afternoon sample or during acute illness.**
> **Testosterone replacement requires a genuine diagnosis, not just symptoms and a borderline level**, and carries risks including erythrocytosis, **suppression of spermatogenesis and infertility** (which must be discussed with men who may want children), and prostate considerations.

**Ix:** **Total testosterone, SHBG and calculated free androgen index** (*why:* quantifies androgen excess, and **a markedly elevated testosterone points to a tumour rather than PCOS**; *what:* degree of elevation). **DHEAS** (*why:* an adrenal androgen — **a markedly raised DHEAS points to an adrenal source**; *what:* elevation). **Early-morning 17-hydroxyprogesterone** (*why:* screens for non-classical congenital adrenal hyperplasia, which mimics PCOS; *what:* elevation). **Prolactin, TFTs, LH and FSH** (*why:* alternative endocrine causes of oligomenorrhoea; *what:* the pattern). Cortisol screening where cushingoid (*why:* Cushing syndrome; *what:* cortisol excess). **Pelvic ultrasound and adrenal imaging where testosterone or DHEAS is markedly raised or virilisation is present** (*why:* localises a tumour; *what:* ovarian or adrenal mass). **Metabolic screen in PCOS — OGTT or HbA1c, lipids, LFT, blood pressure** (*why:* the metabolic consequences are the ones that shorten life; *what:* impaired glucose tolerance, dyslipidaemia, MASLD).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable, except neonatal salt-wasting CAH.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
**PCOS: weight management (the single most effective intervention for both metabolic and reproductive outcomes), combined oral contraceptive for cycle regulation, endometrial protection and androgenic symptoms, antiandrogens (spironolactone, cyproterone) — with EFFECTIVE CONTRACEPTION, because they are teratogenic and feminise a male fetus — metformin for metabolic features, and ovulation induction for fertility.** Cosmetic measures including laser and electrolysis are legitimate and matter to patients.
**Tumour: surgical resection.**

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Metabolic surveillance and endometrial protection in PCOS.** Fertility counselling. **Antiandrogen effects take months to become apparent because of the hair growth cycle — warn the patient, or they will conclude the treatment failed and stop it.**

---

## 0.7 Adrenal and Pituitary Incidentalomas

> [!tip] Two questions for every adrenal incidentaloma
> **1. Is it FUNCTIONAL?**
> **2. Is it MALIGNANT?**
> Everything else follows from those.

> [!warning] The functional workup, which is required in ALL adrenal incidentalomas
> **· Overnight dexamethasone suppression test** — for autonomous cortisol secretion, which is common and, even when subclinical, is associated with hypertension, diabetes, osteoporosis and cardiovascular events.
> **· Plasma free or urinary metanephrines** — for phaeochromocytoma. **Mandatory in every case, regardless of blood pressure, because a proportion are normotensive.**
> **· Aldosterone-to-renin ratio** — **if hypertensive or hypokalaemic.**
> **· Androgens (DHEAS, testosterone)** — **if there are virilising features**, which raise the possibility of adrenocortical carcinoma.
> `UNVERIFIED — the full recommended panel and thresholds.`

> [!danger] Never biopsy an adrenal mass before excluding phaeochromocytoma
> **Biopsying an unrecognised phaeochromocytoma can precipitate a catastrophic hypertensive crisis.**
> **Measure metanephrines first, always.**
> And note that **adrenal biopsy is rarely indicated at all** — it does not reliably distinguish adrenocortical adenoma from carcinoma, and its main role is confirming a suspected metastasis in a patient with a known extra-adrenal primary.

> [!tip] Imaging phenotype predicts benignity
> **A small, homogeneous, well-circumscribed lesion with LOW ATTENUATION on unenhanced CT (reflecting high lipid content) and RAPID CONTRAST WASHOUT is characteristic of a benign adenoma.**
> **Features raising concern: large size · irregular margins · heterogeneity · high unenhanced attenuation · slow washout · necrosis or calcification · growth on serial imaging.**
> **Size and imaging phenotype together determine whether surgery or surveillance is appropriate.** `UNVERIFIED — the attenuation and size thresholds.`
> **Bilateral adrenal masses** suggest metastases, infiltration, haemorrhage, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, or bilateral disease — and warrant assessment of adrenal FUNCTION for insufficiency as well as excess.

> [!tip] Pituitary incidentaloma
> The same two questions apply, plus a third:
> **1. Is it hypersecreting?** — prolactin, IGF-1, cortisol screening.
> **2. Is there hypopituitarism?** — full anterior pituitary profile.
> **3. Is there mass effect?** — **formal visual field testing if the lesion approaches or abuts the optic chiasm.**
> **Microadenomas that are non-functioning and not near the chiasm are generally monitored; macroadenomas require closer surveillance and often intervention.** `UNVERIFIED — surveillance intervals.`

**Ix:** As set out above. **Dedicated adrenal protocol CT or MRI** (*why:* the standard scan may not include the unenhanced and washout phases needed to characterise the lesion, and the patient may need re-imaging; *what:* attenuation, washout, morphology). Serial imaging where surveillance is chosen (*why:* growth changes the assessment; *what:* interval change). Assessment for an extra-adrenal primary where metastasis is plausible (*why:* an adrenal metastasis changes the diagnosis entirely; *what:* primary tumour).

### 0.7.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable.

### 0.7.2 Mx – Definitive
Adrenalectomy for functioning lesions, for those with concerning imaging features, and for large lesions. Surveillance for small non-functioning lesions with benign imaging characteristics.

### 0.7.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**After unilateral adrenalectomy for a cortisol-secreting lesion, the contralateral gland is suppressed and glucocorticoid replacement with sick day rules is required until recovery** — the same principle as after treating Cushing syndrome in 0.4.

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Adrenal crisis → [[F0.3]] 0.6 · Primary aldosteronism and phaeochromocytoma in the hypertension context → [[B2]] 0.4 · Hypertensive emergency → [[B2]] 0.3 · Thyroid axis and giving hydrocortisone before thyroxine → [[I1]] 0.1, 0.4 · Diabetes, steroid-induced hyperglycaemia → [[I2]] 0.7 · Calcium and bone → [[I3]] · Obesity, weight and lipids → [[I5]] · Hyponatraemia, SIADH and diabetes insipidus → [[H3]] · Pituitary apoplexy and thunderclap headache → [[D1]] 0.2 · Cavernous sinus and cranial nerves → [[D7]] 0.6 · Bitemporal hemianopia → [[E2]] · Checkpoint inhibitor endocrinopathy → [[A5]] 0.7 · Osteoporosis and steroid-induced bone loss → [[GER1]] 0.6 · PCOS, menstrual disorders and endometrial protection → [[O4]] · Contraception and teratogenic antiandrogens → [[O6]] · Congenital adrenal hyperplasia in the neonate → [[M3]] · Proximal myopathy → [[B6]] 0.6

## 0.1 Hypothyroidism

**D:** clinical disorder secondary to decreased T3 and T4.

**Causes of primary hypothyroidism:**
- Hashimoto's (autoimmune) — anti-thyroid peroxidase and anti-thyroglobulin antibodies; associated with other autoimmune disorders
- Subacute thyroiditis (de Quervain's) — thought to occur after viral infections; 4 phases: 1st phase (3–6 weeks) with ↑T, painful goitre, ↑ESR; 2nd phase euthyroid; 3rd phase ↓T; 4th phase normal. Scan shows ↓uptake of iodine-131. Usually self-limiting.
- Riedel thyroiditis — chronic inflammation and fibrosis of the thyroid gland
- Post-thyroidectomy or post-radioiodine treatment
- Post-partum thyroiditis
- Drug therapy (e.g. lithium, amiodarone, anti-thyroid drugs)
- Iodine deficiency (dietary)

**Causes of secondary hypothyroidism** (don't directly affect the thyroid gland): Down's syndrome, Turner's syndrome, coeliac disease

**S/Smx** (body slows down):
- General: weight gain, lethargy, cold intolerance
- Skin: dry, cold skin; non-pitting oedema (hands/face — can present as periorbital oedema); dry, coarse scalp hair; loss of the lateral aspect of the eyebrows

> [!info] Gap-filled — "non-pitting" appears three times across this file's hypothyroid/myxoedema content (here, in pretibial myxoedema below, and in Myxoedema Coma below) with no mechanism ever given for why this specific oedema doesn't pit, unlike the overwhelming majority of oedema causes covered elsewhere in this project (heart failure, renal, hepatic, venous). **Mechanism:** ordinary oedema (pitting) is simple excess *fluid* accumulating in the interstitial space, which can be temporarily displaced by finger pressure, leaving a transient indentation. **Myxoedema is fundamentally different** — hypothyroidism causes accumulation of **glycosaminoglycans (GAGs, particularly hyaluronic acid)** in the dermis and subcutaneous tissue, thought to result from reduced GAG clearance/breakdown in the hypothyroid state. These GAGs are hydrophilic and osmotically draw water into the tissue, but the water becomes bound within a thickened, gel-like GAG-rich matrix rather than existing as free interstitial fluid — this matrix doesn't displace under pressure the way free fluid does, so **finger pressure leaves no persistent indentation**, distinguishing it clinically from ordinary fluid-based oedema. This is the same underlying process (just occurring in different locations and different underlying thyroid states) as the pretibial myxoedema associated with Graves' disease below, not repeated here.

- GI: constipation
- Gynae: menorrhagia
- Neuro: carpal tunnel syndrome

> [!info] Gap-filled — **Woltman's sign** (delayed relaxation phase of a deep tendon reflex, classically the ankle/Achilles reflex — a "hung-up" or "slow-relaxing" reflex) is a genuinely classic hypothyroidism finding that was completely absent above. **Mechanism:** thyroid hormone normally supports rapid calcium reuptake into the sarcoplasmic reticulum after a muscle contraction, along with adequate myosin ATPase activity and ATP turnover — hypothyroidism slows both of these processes, so the *contraction* phase of the reflex remains relatively preserved while the **relaxation phase is specifically and disproportionately prolonged**, producing the "hung-up" quality on examination (the foot returns to resting position noticeably slower than normal, rather than a simple diminished/absent reflex). This is genuinely a useful examination finding to actively test for, not just a historical curiosity — though it isn't specific to hypothyroidism alone, given a similar delayed-relaxation pattern is also described in anorexia nervosa, advanced age, and hypothermia, so it should be interpreted alongside the other findings above rather than in isolation.

**Ix:** TFTs (*why:* the diagnostic test — establishes both the diagnosis and whether it's primary or secondary based on the TSH/T4 pattern; *what:* primary hypothyroidism shows ↑TSH with ↓free T4; secondary (pituitary/hypothalamic) hypothyroidism shows inappropriately low/normal TSH despite ↓free T4 — this pattern distinction is important, since secondary hypothyroidism warrants pituitary/hypothalamic investigation rather than simply starting levothyroxine and monitoring TSH the way primary disease is managed). Anti-thyroid peroxidase and anti-thyroglobulin antibodies if Hashimoto's suspected (*why:* confirms the autoimmune aetiology, the most common cause; *what:* positive in the majority of Hashimoto's cases).

> [!info] Verified — the levothyroxine starting doses, cautious titration in IHD, 8–12 week TFT recheck interval, and TSH target (0.5–2.5 mU/L) reflect standard international endocrine practice rather than a UK-specific protocol; carbimazole (not methimazole) is confirmed as the standard first-line antithyroid drug in Australia too, consistent with the Graves' Mx below. No material correction needed to the figures in this section.
> **Mx:** levothyroxine 50–100 mcg OD (if >50yo with IHD, start with 25 mcg OD and slowly titrate up). Check TFTs 8–12 weeks after any dose change. Goal is to normalise TSH — aim for 0.5–2.5 mU/L. In pregnancy, increase dose by ≥25–50 mcg, monitor TSH. **Practical administration point (an Australian endocrinology source specifically emphasises this):** take on an empty stomach with water only, at least 30 minutes before food, separately from other medications — absorption is significantly affected by food/other drugs, which is a common cause of apparently "resistant" hypothyroidism that's actually an absorption/adherence issue.

**Levothyroxine SE:** hyperthyroidism, ↓bone mineral density (may need calcium + vitamin D supplementation), worsening angina, AF.

> [!warning] In amiodarone-induced hypothyroidism, continue amiodarone + give levothyroxine.

> [!danger] Interactions: iron and calcium carbonate decrease levothyroxine absorption — give ≥4h apart. Levothyroxine is safe in breastfeeding.

### 0.1.1 Subclinical Hypothyroidism
= ↑TSH but normal T3, T4 + no obvious symptoms. Possible in patients non-compliant with levothyroxine.

> [!info] Verified — the TSH thresholds below (treat if >10, individualise 5.5–10 based on symptoms/age) reflect broad international consensus rather than a UK-specific set of numbers; multiple international sources converge on similar thresholds (treatment more clearly indicated >10 mIU/L, individualised judgment below that based on symptoms, age, and risk factors). No material correction needed.
> **Mx:**
> - TSH >10 mU/L on 2 occasions 3 months apart (T3/T4 normal) → consider offering levothyroxine
> - TSH 5.5–10 mU/L on 2 occasions 3 months apart + symptomatic + <65yo → consider a 6-month trial of levothyroxine
> - TSH 5.5–10 mU/L in older people → watch and wait
> - TSH 5.5–10 mU/L + asymptomatic → observe, repeat TFT in 6 months

### 0.1.2 Sick Euthyroid Syndrome
= non-thyroidal illness with ↓TSH, ↓T3, ↓T4. Most of the time TSH is roughly in the normal range ("inappropriate", since TSH should be high if T3/T4 are low). Usually a result of systemic illness. No treatment needed if it reverses upon recovery.

### 0.1.3 Myxoedema Coma
= extremely decompensated hypothyroidism.

**S/Smx:** confusion, hypothermia.

**Ix:** TFTs (*why:* confirms severe hypothyroidism as the cause; *what:* markedly deranged, often with very low T4); cortisol (*why:* screens for coexisting adrenal insufficiency, since giving thyroid hormone alone without covering a coexisting cortisol deficiency can precipitate an adrenal crisis — this is why the Mx below always pairs thyroid replacement with empirical corticosteroid cover; *what:* may be low if coexisting adrenal insufficiency present); U&Es, glucose, blood cultures/septic screen (*why:* myxoedema coma is often precipitated by an intercurrent illness/infection, which itself needs identifying and treating; *what:* may reveal the precipitant and associated metabolic derangements — e.g. hyponatraemia, hypoglycaemia).

**Mx:**
- **Immediate/acute:** IV thyroid replacement; IV corticosteroids given empirically alongside (possibility of coexisting adrenal insufficiency — don't wait for cortisol results, given how dangerous unopposed thyroid replacement can be in this scenario); IV fluids, correct electrolytes; identify and treat any precipitating illness.
- **Chronic/long-term:** transition to oral levothyroxine replacement once stabilised, with ongoing monitoring as for standard hypothyroidism Mx above.

---

## 0.2 Hyperthyroidism

**D:** clinical state secondary to increased T3 and T4. "Thyrotoxicosis" is an interchangeable term.

**Causes:**
- Graves' disease (most common)
- Toxic nodular goitre
- Postpartum thyroiditis
- Gestational hyperthyroidism
- TSH-producing pituitary adenoma
- Thyroid cancers (don't usually cause hyper- or hypothyroidism)
- Iodine-induced hyperthyroidism (diet, contrast, drugs e.g. amiodarone)

**S/Smx:** weight loss, sweating, heat intolerance, tremor, irritability; palpitations ± AF; diarrhoea; oligomenorrhoea.

> [!info] Gap-filled — lid lag/lid retraction was mentioned only as a bare term elsewhere in this project (in the Ocular Manifestations reference), never explained, and never distinguished from exophthalmos despite the two being genuinely different processes that are easy to conflate as "the same thyroid eye thing." **Lid lag/retraction: sympathetic overactivity, not autoimmune.** Hyperthyroidism's generalised increase in sympathetic/adrenergic tone causes overactivity of **Müller's muscle** — a smooth muscle assisting upper eyelid elevation, under sympathetic control — producing lid retraction at rest and a visible lag of the upper lid behind the globe during downward eye movement. **This occurs in *any* cause of thyrotoxicosis** (Graves', toxic nodular goitre, thyroiditis, exogenous excess), given it simply reflects generalised sympathetic overactivity, not an eye-specific disease process — it resolves once the hyperthyroid state itself is corrected. This is genuinely distinct from **exophthalmos/proptosis**, which is specifically an autoimmune process (retro-orbital glycosaminoglycan and collagen deposition — see Thyroid Eye Disease in [[05_Ophthalmology]], not repeated here) occurring specifically in Graves' disease, and which — importantly — **doesn't reliably correlate with or resolve alongside thyroid hormone status**, unlike lid lag. Confusing the two matters clinically: lid lag in a hyperthyroid patient of any cause is expected and non-specific, while exophthalmos should prompt specific consideration of Graves'-associated thyroid eye disease and its own distinct, independently-progressing management pathway.

> [!note] Amiodarone can cause both hyper- and hypothyroidism.

### 0.2.1 Graves' Disease

**D:** autoimmune thyroid condition associated with hyperthyroidism — antibodies to the TSH receptor (90%) and anti-thyroid peroxidase antibodies (75%).

**R:** family history, F>M (6:1), tobacco use

**Features specific to Graves':**
- Eyes (30%): exophthalmos and ophthalmoplegia (see Ophthalmology section) — smoking increases risk of eye disease
- Pretibial myxoedema — scaly, lumpy, non-pitting skin on the shins
- Thyroid acropachy — digital clubbing, soft tissue swelling of hands and feet, periosteal new bone formation

**Ix:** thyroid scintigraphy scan (*why:* distinguishes Graves' disease from other causes of hyperthyroidism by its characteristic uptake pattern, particularly useful when clinical features alone don't clearly indicate Graves'; *what:* diffuse, homogeneous, increased uptake of iodine-131 — contrast with the patchy uptake of toxic multinodular goitre below). TSH receptor antibodies (*why:* a specific serological test supporting the diagnosis, particularly useful when scintigraphy is unavailable or contraindicated e.g. in pregnancy; *what:* positive in ~90% of Graves' patients).

> [!info] Verified — carbimazole (not methimazole) is confirmed as the standard first-line antithyroid drug in Australia, and the propranolol-for-symptoms → carbimazole → radioiodine stepwise sequence reflects standard international practice, not a UK-specific protocol. No material correction needed.
> **Mx:**
> - **Immediate/acute (symptom control):** propranolol for symptom control while awaiting specialist review/definitive Mx.
> - **Definitive:** refer to endocrine; likely to start carbimazole if symptoms not controlled with propranolol; if still refractory, radioiodine therapy may be considered (contraindicated if pregnant or <16yo); surgery (thyroidectomy) is a further definitive option for select patients (e.g. large goitre, patient preference, or when antithyroid drugs/radioiodine are contraindicated/declined).
> - **Chronic/long-term:** patients who undergo radioiodine or surgery will typically become hypothyroid and require lifelong levothyroxine replacement (see Hypothyroidism above) — this is an expected outcome of definitive treatment, not a complication, and should be explained to patients as part of informed consent for these options.

**Anti-thyroid drugs (e.g. carbimazole):** MOA — inhibits thyroid peroxidase.

> [!danger] SE: agranulocytosis — warn patients to watch for signs of infection.

### 0.2.2 Toxic Multinodular Goitre
= multiple autonomously functioning thyroid nodules.

**Ix:** thyroid scintigraphy scan (*why:* distinguishes this from Graves' disease by the uptake pattern; *what:* patchy, heterogeneous uptake — contrast with the diffuse uptake of Graves' above).

**Mx:** radioiodine therapy is the mainstay definitive treatment — carbimazole can be used for interim symptom control but autonomous nodules don't remit the way Graves' disease can, so long-term antithyroid drug therapy alone isn't a definitive strategy the way it can be trialled in Graves'.

### 0.2.3 Thyroid Storm (Thyrotoxic Crisis)
Life-threatening hyperthyroidism, precipitated by surgery, trauma, infection or acute illness, acute iodine overload (e.g. contrast).

**S/Smx:** fever (>38.5°C); CNS dysfunction (agitation, delirium, psychosis, lethargy, seizure, coma); ↑HR ± AF, heart failure; GI dysfunction (nausea/vomiting/diarrhoea, abdominal pain, unexplained jaundice).

**Ix:** clinical diagnosis (often using a scoring system such as the **Burch-Wartofsky Point Scale**, which weights thermoregulatory, cardiovascular, gastrointestinal-hepatic and central nervous system dysfunction plus the presence of a precipitant — **the intern-level point is that it supports a clinical decision already made on the bedside picture, and a low score does not exclude thyroid storm in a patient who looks like one**; the full weighted scoring is specialist territory and is not reproduced here) supported by TFTs (*why:* thyroid storm is primarily a clinical diagnosis given the need for immediate treatment — waiting for TFT confirmation shouldn't delay Mx if the clinical picture is convincing; *what:* markedly elevated T3/T4 with suppressed TSH, though the degree of biochemical derangement doesn't necessarily correlate with clinical severity).

**Mx:**
- **Immediate/acute:** IV propranolol (β-blockade) for symptomatic/haemodynamic control; antithyroid drugs — **carbimazole (or propylthiouracil, particularly in pregnancy given carbimazole's teratogenicity, or if hepatotoxicity is a specific concern)** — corrected here from the source's "methimazole," which is the equivalent US drug name for the same active moiety carbimazole is converted to in the body, but carbimazole is the actual product prescribed in Australia and the UK; dexamethasone (blocks conversion of T4 to T3, and also covers possible coexisting adrenal insufficiency, similar to the rationale in myxoedema coma above); Lugol's iodine (given at least 1 hour after starting antithyroid drugs, to avoid providing substrate for new hormone synthesis before the antithyroid drug has blocked the synthetic pathway); symptomatic treatment (antipyretics avoiding aspirin, which displaces thyroid hormone from binding proteins and can worsen the picture — paracetamol preferred); treat the precipitating event.
- **Chronic/long-term:** once stabilised, transition to standard Graves'/hyperthyroidism Mx pathway above, including consideration of definitive therapy to prevent recurrence.

---

## 0.3 Thyroid Cancers

| Type | Features |
|---|---|
| **Papillary carcinoma (70%)** | F>M (3:1), young patients, good prognosis. Cancer of epithelial follicular cells, seldom encapsulated. Metastasises usually via lymph nodes, rarely haematogenously. |
| **Follicular adenoma** | Benign encapsulated tumour, usually a solitary thyroid nodule; requires histology to exclude malignancy. |
| **Follicular carcinoma (20%)** | Malignant transformation of follicular adenoma; capsular invasion (usually vascular); multifocal disease rare. |
| **Medullary carcinoma (5%)** | Malignancy of parafollicular C cells (neural crest derived) — secretes calcitonin. Associated with MEN2 (~20% familial). Nodal disease is associated with very poor prognosis. |
| **Anaplastic carcinoma (1%)** | Aka undifferentiated thyroid carcinoma. Highly aggressive, very poor prognosis, most common in elderly females. Mx: resection where possible, palliation via isthmusectomy and radiotherapy (chemo not effective). |
| **Thyroid lymphoma (rare)** | Associated with Hashimoto's. Primary — affects thyroid first, then LN, then other organs (B-cell lymphoma most common). Secondary — affects LN then other organs then thyroid. |

---

## 0.4 Goitre

**D:** neck swelling due to a thyroid issue. 5–12% malignant; usually benign and asymptomatic. Both hyper- and hypothyroidism can cause goitre.

**Dx:** establish whether the mass is intra- or extra-thyroidal — US + examination. Evaluate lymph nodes also. The thyroid moves with swallowing.

**Ix:** TFTs (*why:* establishes whether the goitre is associated with hyper-, hypo-, or euthyroid status, which narrows the underlying cause; *what:* may be deranged in either direction depending on cause, or normal in a simple/multinodular goitre). Thyroid US (*why:* characterises the goitre's structure — diffuse vs multinodular vs single dominant nodule — and identifies any nodule features raising malignancy concern warranting biopsy; *what:* diffuse enlargement suggests Graves'/Hashimoto's, multiple nodules suggest multinodular goitre, a single dominant or suspicious nodule warrants fine-needle aspiration). Fine-needle aspiration if a suspicious nodule is identified (*why:* the key test to exclude malignancy in a dominant/suspicious nodule, given the 5–12% malignancy rate above; *what:* cytology guides further Mx — see Thyroid Cancers above for the malignant subtypes this may reveal).

**Mx:**
- **Definitive:** treat the underlying cause (see Hypothyroidism, Hyperthyroidism, and Thyroid Cancers above/below for the specific disease-level Mx this feeds into) — goitre itself is a sign, not a standalone disease, so Mx is directed at the cause once identified.
- **Chronic/long-term:** large goitres causing compressive symptoms (dysphagia, dyspnoea, stridor) may need surgical referral for thyroidectomy regardless of the underlying functional status, given the mechanical rather than purely hormonal problem this represents.

---

## 0.15 Diabetes Mellitus

### 0.15.1 Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM)

**D:** metabolic disorder characterised by hyperglycaemia due to absolute insulin deficiency.

**A/P:** autoimmune or idiopathic destruction of pancreatic β cells.

**S/Smx:** peak diagnosis age 10–14; hyperglycaemia, polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, increased fatigue. In adults: hyperglycaemia, ketosis, rapid weight loss, BMI <25, often with family history.

> [!danger] Some patients present with DKA as their first presentation.

**Dx criteria for diabetes mellitus:**
- Symptomatic patients: random plasma glucose ≥11.1 mmol/L
- Fasting plasma glucose ≥7.0 mmol/L
- Plasma glucose ≥11.1 mmol/L 2h after 75g OGTT
- HbA1c ≥48 mmol/mol (≥6.5%)

**Ix:** glucose levels (*why:* confirms hyperglycaemia against the diagnostic criteria above; *what:* elevated per the criteria). Plasma or urine ketones (*why:* screens for ketosis, both supporting the T1DM diagnosis (absolute insulin deficiency drives ketogenesis) and screening for DKA at presentation, given how commonly T1DM first presents this way; *what:* positive/elevated ketones support T1DM over T2DM and may indicate concurrent DKA). C-peptide (*why:* directly reflects endogenous insulin production, distinguishing T1DM (absolute deficiency) from T2DM (relative deficiency with preserved/excess endogenous production) — particularly useful in atypical presentations where the clinical picture alone doesn't clearly distinguish the two; *what:* low, reflecting minimal endogenous insulin production). Diabetes-specific autoantibodies (*why:* confirms the autoimmune subtype specifically, supporting T1DM diagnosis and distinguishing from other causes of absolute insulin deficiency; *what:* e.g. anti-GAD, anti-IA2, anti-insulin antibodies — positive in the majority of autoimmune T1DM).

**Mx:** basal-bolus insulin (long-acting for basal, rapid-acting for bolus) in a multiple daily injection regimen. Metformin if BMI ≥25. Monitor glucose before each meal and before bed (≥4x daily); more frequent monitoring if unwell, exercising more, or pregnant.

> [!info] Verified — Australian obstetric practice confirms low-dose aspirin for pre-eclampsia prevention in high-risk pregnancies (including pre-existing diabetes), starting 12–16 weeks and continuing until 36 weeks, consistent with the general principle in the UK-sourced note — this is genuinely international-consensus practice (WHO and multiple countries including Australia recommend it), not a UK-only protocol. Two AU-relevant refinements: **RANZCOG guidance and Australian obstetric units generally use 100–150mg (rather than 75mg)**, and **night-time dosing is now preferred** over morning dosing, since it aligns better with circadian platelet activity/BP patterns and shows greater BP reduction/endothelial benefit in trial evidence — a specific, practical point not in the original note.
> Pregnant women with T1DM — low-dose aspirin (100–150mg, taken at night) from 12–16 weeks until 36 weeks.

### 0.15.2 Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2DM)

**D:** progressive disorder defined by deficits in insulin secretion and increased insulin resistance, leading to abnormal glucose metabolism and related metabolic derangements.

**A/P:** relative insulin deficiency due to excess adipose tissue — not enough insulin to "go around" the excess fatty tissue.

**S/Smx:** polyuria, polydipsia ± weight loss, paraesthesias, acanthosis nigricans. Usually picked up incidentally.

**R:** ↑age, obesity, PMH of GDM, prediabetes, family history of T2DM, non-white ancestry, PCOS, HTN, dyslipidaemia, CVD, stress.

> [!danger] A genuine, severe Australian health equity issue with a specific, actionable screening-tool exception — not a generic epidemiological footnote. **T2DM in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Australians is roughly 4 times more prevalent than in non-Indigenous Australians, with a substantially earlier age of onset** (T2DM was traditionally considered an adult-onset disease, but is now diagnosed in Indigenous children as young as 10–14) and diabetes-related death occurring at a median age around **20 years younger** than non-Indigenous Australians — diabetes was the leading cause of death for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander women in 2023.
> - **The critical, actionable point: AUSDRISK (Australia's standard diabetes risk-assessment tool) is not recommended for use in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people**, given the significantly higher baseline prevalence in these communities means the tool can genuinely underestimate individual risk — directly analogous to how Centor/FeverPAIN don't capture rheumatic fever risk (see [[13_05a_ENT_-_Sore_Throat_and_Tonsillitis]], not repeated here) — a general-population risk-stratification tool doesn't transfer safely to a population with a fundamentally different baseline risk.
> - **Current RACGP guidance recommends bypassing the risk-calculator approach entirely** and instead performing **annual screening** (fasting glucose, random glucose, or HbA1c) directly — from age 15 as part of the MBS-funded Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander health check, or from age 10 in remote settings if additional risk factors (overweight, family history, maternal GDM, PCOS, acanthosis nigricans) are present. This is a genuinely earlier and more proactive screening threshold than general population practice, not just the same test done slightly more often.

**Ix:** fasting lipid profile (*why:* T2DM carries significant cardiovascular risk, so lipid assessment is part of the baseline cardiovascular risk stratification driving Mx decisions — see `I5 §0.3 Lipid Disorders` above and the T2DM Stepwise Drug Management section below for how this feeds into treatment choice; *what:* often shows the characteristic diabetic dyslipidaemia pattern — ↑triglycerides, ↓HDL). Urine ACR (*why:* screens for diabetic nephropathy, one of the key microvascular complications, and its presence/severity influences both cardiorenal drug choice (e.g. SGLT2i) and monitoring intensity; *what:* elevated ACR indicates albuminuria/nephropathy). eGFR (*why:* assesses renal function, relevant both to nephropathy screening and to drug dosing/choice (e.g. metformin contraindications, SGLT2i eligibility); *what:* may show reduced function in established nephropathy). ECG (*why:* baseline cardiovascular assessment given the elevated CV risk in T2DM; *what:* may reveal silent ischaemia or baseline abnormalities relevant to future risk assessment). ABPI (*why:* screens for peripheral vascular disease, a macrovascular complication with implications for foot care and vascular risk; *what:* reduced ABPI indicates PVD). LFT (*why:* baseline before starting medications metabolised hepatically or with hepatotoxic potential, and screens for non-alcoholic fatty liver disease, which is strongly associated with T2DM; *what:* may show evidence of NAFLD/steatohepatitis).

**Mx:** lifestyle modification (diet, exercise, stop smoking), management of comorbidities, plus pharmacological agents (see T2DM Stepwise Drug Management below for the full AU-verified approach, not repeated here).

### 0.15.3 Complications of Diabetes Mellitus

**Macrovascular:**
- Brain — stroke/TIA, cognitive impairment
- Heart — HTN, atherosclerosis, CHD, MI
- Extremities — peripheral vascular disease, increased risk of ulcers, gangrene, amputation

**Microvascular:**
- Eye — diabetic retinopathy, cataracts, glaucoma (see [[05_Ophthalmology]] Diabetic Retinopathy, Cataracts, and Open-Angle Glaucoma for the full AU-verified disease-level detail on each, not repeated here)
- Kidney — diabetic nephropathy → CKD
- Neuropathy — peripheral neuropathy (glove and stocking pattern), contributes to extremity issues (see Diabetic Foot below for how this and the macrovascular extremity complications combine into a distinct clinical entity)

### 0.15.4 Diabetic Foot

> [!note] Gap-filled from CSV ("Diabetic foot," High yield) — not covered in the source notes despite being one of the highest-yield diabetes complications topics, combining elements of the neuropathy and peripheral vascular disease already listed above into a distinct clinical entity with its own assessment and management approach. See [[04_Neurology]] Diabetic Neuropathy for the fuller classification and Mx of the neuropathic component specifically (including the pharmacological approach to painful neuropathy), not repeated here.

**D:** a spectrum of foot pathology in people with diabetes arising from the combination of peripheral neuropathy, peripheral arterial disease, and increased infection susceptibility — the leading cause of non-traumatic lower limb amputation.

**A/P:** three interacting mechanisms, often present together in the same patient:
- **Peripheral neuropathy** (sensory, motor, and autonomic) — sensory loss means injury goes unnoticed; motor neuropathy causes small muscle wasting and altered foot biomechanics (clawing of toes, altered pressure points); autonomic neuropathy causes reduced sweating (dry, cracked skin prone to fissuring and infection) and arteriovenous shunting.
- **Peripheral arterial disease** — reduces tissue perfusion, impairing wound healing and increasing infection/ischaemic risk once a wound occurs.
- **Increased infection susceptibility** — hyperglycaemia impairs neutrophil function and wound healing generally, so infections that would be trivial in a non-diabetic patient can progress rapidly.

**S/Smx:**
- **Neuropathic ulcer:** typically painless (given the sensory loss), located over pressure points (plantar surface, metatarsal heads, heel), surrounded by callus, in a foot that may otherwise feel warm with palpable pulses (neuropathy without significant ischaemia).
- **Ischaemic ulcer:** typically painful, located at the foot margins/toes, in a foot that is cool with reduced/absent pulses — often with other signs of PVD (see [[01_Cardiovascular]] Peripheral Arterial Disease (PAD), not repeated here).
- **Neuroischaemic ulcer:** features of both — increasingly the most common pattern given how often neuropathy and PVD coexist.
- **Charcot foot (neuroarthropathy):** a specific and easily-missed complication — acute presentation is a warm, swollen, sometimes painful foot (which can be mistaken for cellulitis or gout given active inflammation) in a patient with established neuropathy, progressing to bone/joint destruction and characteristic foot deformity ("rocker-bottom foot") if unrecognised and not offloaded — a genuine diagnostic pitfall given how easily it's mistaken for infection when in fact it's an inflammatory/destructive bone process requiring offloading rather than antibiotics as the primary intervention.
- **Infection:** cellulitis, abscess, osteomyelitis — signs of infection can be subtle in a neuropathic/ischaemic foot (reduced systemic inflammatory response), so a high index of suspicion is needed; probe-to-bone testing (a sterile probe reaching bone through an ulcer) is a useful bedside sign strongly suggestive of underlying osteomyelitis.

**Ix:** clinical foot examination — inspection (deformity, callus, ulceration, skin changes), palpation (temperature, pulses), 10g monofilament testing and vibration sense (*why:* the standard bedside screen for peripheral sensory neuropathy, identifying patients at high ulceration risk before an ulcer develops — this is the basis of the annual diabetic foot screening/risk stratification programs; *what:* loss of monofilament/vibration sensation indicates high-risk neuropathic feet). ABPI (*why:* screens for coexisting PVD, relevant both to healing potential and to revascularisation decisions; *what:* reduced ABPI indicates arterial insufficiency — see [[01_Cardiovascular]] for the fuller ABPI interpretation, not repeated here). Wound swab/deep tissue culture if infected (*why:* identifies the causative organism(s), often polymicrobial in diabetic foot infections, to guide targeted antibiotic therapy; *what:* guides antibiotic choice). X-ray ± MRI if osteomyelitis suspected (*why:* MRI is more sensitive for early osteomyelitis than X-ray, which may not show changes until bone destruction is more advanced; *what:* MRI shows bone marrow oedema/destruction; X-ray may show periosteal reaction or bone destruction in more established disease).

**Mx:**
- **Immediate/acute (infected ulcer):** antibiotics per local guidelines (often broad-spectrum given the polymicrobial nature of diabetic foot infections), urgent surgical debridement if significant infection/abscess/necrotic tissue, urgent vascular review if critical limb ischaemia is present given the time-sensitivity of revascularisation.
- **Definitive (uninfected ulcer):** offloading (the single most important intervention for a neuropathic ulcer — pressure removal from the affected area, e.g. via a total contact cast or offloading footwear, allows healing that won't otherwise occur despite good wound care alone), wound debridement and appropriate dressings, glycaemic optimisation, revascularisation if significant PVD is limiting healing.
- **Charcot foot specifically:** offloading/immobilisation (total contact casting) is the primary treatment — recognising this as a distinct entity from infection is critical, since inappropriately treating it as cellulitis with antibiotics alone, without offloading, allows ongoing joint destruction and deformity.
- **Chronic/long-term (prevention — arguably the most important tier for this condition):** regular podiatry review and foot screening (annual at minimum, more frequent if high-risk), patient education on daily foot inspection (particularly important given the sensory loss means patients cannot rely on pain to alert them to a problem), appropriate footwear, prompt review of any new lesion.

**P:** diabetic foot ulceration carries significant morbidity (recurrence is common even after healing) and is the leading cause of non-traumatic lower limb amputation — prevention through regular screening and patient education is disproportionately more effective than treatment once ulceration has occurred.


### 0.15.5 T2DM Stepwise Drug Management

> [!info] Verified against RACGP's 2024 "Management of Type 2 Diabetes" handbook and recent PBS changes (Medicine Today, June 2025), Aug 2026 — **the Australian approach has genuinely diverged from the sequential "metformin first, then add" UK-style model since PBS changes in June 2024, December 2024, and April 2025.** This is a substantive, currently-relevant difference, not just a drug-name or dose adjustment.
> **The key shift:** metformin is **no longer necessarily the mandatory default first-line agent**. As of April 2025, PBS rules allow **any SGLT2 inhibitor to be used as initial combination therapy WITH metformin from treatment start** (not added sequentially after a metformin trial) for patients who are at high cardiovascular risk (≥10% 5-year risk via the Australian CVD risk calculator, cvdcheck.org.au), have established atherosclerotic CVD, chronic kidney disease, or heart failure — and critically, **there is no longer an HbA1c threshold requirement** before this combination can be used (previously HbA1c needed to be >7% after a metformin trial; this restriction has been removed). This reflects a philosophical shift in Australian diabetes care from "glycaemic control first, cardiorenal risk second" toward upfront cardiorenal risk stratification driving initial drug choice.
> **What this means practically:** for a newly-diagnosed T2DM patient, the first clinical step is no longer simply "start metformin" — it now requires assessing cardiovascular/renal/heart-failure risk status *before* deciding whether metformin alone or metformin+SGLT2i combination is the appropriate starting point. A patient with high CV risk may reasonably start on combination therapy from diagnosis, even with a relatively modest HbA1c, given the drugs' cardiorenal protective benefits are now prioritised independent of glycaemic control alone.
> **The general escalation logic in the UK-sourced steps below** (add further agents if HbA1c remains above target; insulin if triple therapy fails) is still broadly reasonable as a fallback teaching framework, but should not be taught as the primary Australian first-line pathway given the above — the starting point itself is now risk-stratified, not purely metformin-then-escalate.
> 1. Metformin (if not tolerated or contraindicated: SGLT2 inhibitor alone, or DPP4 inhibitor + pioglitazone/sulfonylurea) — **or metformin + SGLT2i as initial combination if high CV/renal/HF risk, per the above.**
> 2. If HbA1c >58 mmol/mol (7.5%) — add sulfonylurea, gliptin (DPP4i), pioglitazone, or SGLT2 inhibitor
> 3. If HbA1c still >58 — add a 2nd or 3rd drug from above, or start insulin
> 4. If triple therapy not effective/tolerated/contraindicated AND BMI >35 — metformin + sulfonylurea + GLP1 mimetic
> 5. If HbA1c still >58 — insulin
>
> Target HbA1c ≤48 mmol/mol (≤53 if on a drug that may cause hypoglycaemia). Only add a new drug if HbA1c >58 mmol/mol. These specific numeric targets are broadly consistent with RACGP guidance (general target <7%, more flexible up to 8% in frail older patients), though the decision to escalate now also depends on cardiorenal risk status, not HbA1c alone.

> [!info] HbA1c reference ranges
> Normal: <42 mmol/mol (<6%) | Pre-diabetes: 42–47 mmol/mol (6–6.4%) | Diabetes: ≥48 mmol/mol (≥6.5%) | Goal for known diabetes: 58 mmol/mol (7.5%) | Impaired fasting glucose: 6.1–6.9 mmol/L

### 0.15.6 DM Sick Day Rules

> [!danger] Do NOT stop insulin — risk of DKA.

Increase monitoring ± check ketones; consume 3L of fluids/24h ± sugary drinks.

> [!info] Verified against RACGP's "Diabetes sick-day management" guidance, Aug 2026 — the drug-specific principles below are directionally confirmed as correct for Australia (RACGP's own document uses the same general logic), with one addition — GLP-1 receptor agonists specifically aggravate nausea/vomiting themselves (not just an AKI-dehydration concern), which is worth knowing as a distinct reason to stop them during GI illness.
> - Metformin: stop if risk of dehydration/GI illness (lactic acidosis risk; may also directly aggravate GI symptoms)
> - Sulfonylureas: caution — increased hypoglycaemia risk; hold only if blood glucose is low, not routinely
> - SGLT2 inhibitors: check ketones, stop if acutely unwell + risk of dehydration (euglycaemic DKA risk) — RACGP specifically advises having a blood ketone meter available for patients on SGLT2i given this risk
> - GLP-1 receptor agonists: stop if risk of dehydration (AKI risk) — **and independently, because they may themselves aggravate nausea/vomiting**, a direct symptomatic reason to stop distinct from the dehydration-risk rationale
> - Seek urgent medical review if blood glucose ≥15 mmol/L on two consecutive readings (2 hours apart) — a specific numeric trigger point from the RACGP guidance worth knowing

### 0.15.7 Perioperative Diabetes Management

> [!info] Resolved — verified against the ADS-ANZCA Perioperative Diabetes and Hyperglycaemia Guidelines (Adults), the actual Australian national guideline co-developed by the Australian Diabetes Society and ANZCA, Aug 2026. This is genuinely more detailed and in places different from the general international principles the file previously described — most notably, basal insulin is **not** routinely reduced the day before surgery (only reduced if there's a specific reason to), and SGLT2i cessation timing is longer than a single "day of" omission for major surgery.
> **Non-insulin medications:**
> - **SGLT2 inhibitors** — withhold for **two days prior to surgery AND the day of surgery** (major surgery/anything requiring bowel preparation); for **minor/day-stay procedures** (including gastroscopy), only the day of the procedure needs SGLT2i held — genuinely longer than a simple "day of" omission for anything beyond minor surgery, reflecting the euglycaemic DKA risk this class carries.
> - **All other non-insulin anti-hyperglycaemic medications** (metformin, DPP4i, sulfonylureas, GLP-1 agonists) — continue as usual up to and including the night before surgery, then **withhold on the day of surgery itself** (not "no adjustment" for metformin/DPP4i/GLP-1 as previously stated) — this is a genuine, deliberate simplification in current Australian practice: essentially all oral/non-insulin diabetes medications are held on the day of surgery regardless of class, rather than picking and choosing which classes need holding.
> **Insulin (except pumps):**
